#3000. Prediction market traders now see nearly 75% chance of Democrats winning the Senate

Momentum for Democrats is building on Kalshi, with traders on Monday seeing a 74% chance of the party winning control of the U.S. Senate — the highest odds seen for the party on the platform since the market first opened.

The higher odds come after The New York Times reported Sunday that a poll it conducted with Siena University found Democrats are leading in several battleground states, strengthening Democrats' path to a possible Senate majority.

The contract asks "Who will control the Senate?" and will be verified by the Library of Congress after Tuesday's vote.

Since it opened in 2024, Republicans dominated the market for Senate control in 2026. The gap between the parties' odds narrowed shortly after the U.S. and Israel first launched strikes on Iran on Feb. 28, though the chances for Republican control jumped up to 60% in July before reversing in mid-September.

Markets on separate Senate races have also seen major movement in favor of Democratic candidates in the last 24 hours. Traders on Kalshi now see a 74% chance of Democrats winning the Senate race in Texas, up from 68.4%, over the past 24 hours. The market on the Senate race in Iowa jumped to 50% from 42% on Polymarket in favor of Democrats.

In Kansas, which has not elected a Democratic U.S. senator since 1932, odds of Democratic candidate Adam Hamilton winning over Republican Roger Marshall are up to 63% from 52%.

Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2996. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2994. Pistons vs. Hornets final score: Cade Cunningham powers second-half surge in blowout win

The Detroit Pistons played great basketball throughout the night, but the Charlotte Hornets just kept hanging around. The Pistons were shooting better than 60% from the floor and 50% from three, and yet a Dennis Schroder layup made it a two-possession game with just under five minutes left in the second quarter. Then Cade Cunningham took over, then the Detroit Pistons took over, and suddenly a squeaker turned into a 124-91 Detroit win.

In that decisive 45-16 run, Cade Cunningham scored 14 points, and he did it in under three and a half minutes of game time. One of the league's best players finally had his first superstar quarter of the season, scoring 17 points and notching three assists in total in that decisive third frame. He finished the night with 33 points, seven assists, and five steals. In those magical few minutes, he was everywhere in his bag. Patient mid-range pull-ups, slicing through the D for layups, catch-and-shoot threes. He went to the bench with 1:17 left in the third quarter, and you were left thinking he might hit 50 in the fourth. But he never needed to come back in the game again.

He saw the game was getting too close, and he decided to do something about it. He put the team on his back and said, "We're playing too well to not blow these guys out."

But the true blowout wouldn't have happened without all the other contributions up and down the lineup. The Pistons really have something on offense this season, and while the range of ball handlers and actions can lead to some self-inflicted wounds, it is designed to open up this offense and let it breathe. And, boy, did they take a big gulping breath versus the Hornets.

The Pistons shot 57% from the floor and 48% from deep, and that was dragged down a bit as a consequence of some extended garbage time. The Pistons also kept their defense in top form. They forced 25 Charlotte turnovers, and that led to 27 fast-break points.

Detroit was led by Cade, but Duncan Robinson continues to be lights out this season. He shot 6-of-9 from deep on his way to 24 points. His stroke was immediate and effortless. He's truly in the zone. Some of that shooting magic might be rubbing off on Kevin Huerter. The one-time bucket has struggled from deep for a few seasons now, including his short stint after a mid-season trade to Detroit last season. But he hit three threes tonight and is now shooting 38% from deep for the season. Even Ron Holland canned three threes tonight. Not to be overlooked is Ausar Thompson's expanded role in the offense.

It's not always perfect, but Thompson is getting more comfortable making decisions with the ball in his hands, and he's understanding how to make defense pay by using his strength and burst to get something close to the basket. Thompson was 5-of-7 from the floor for 14 points, and he added six rebounds and four assists.

The Pistons had plenty of lopsided runs last season in their sprint to 60 wins. But those were driven by a defense that would suffocate you and an offense that could do just enough. Last year, the Pistons could go on a 27-2 run over the course of 17 minutes of game action. Tonight, they went on the same run in just over eight minutes.

Detroit's defense remains the best in the league. Its offense is starting to catch up. That's bad news for everyone else in the NBA.

#2991. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

#2988. Knicks turn it up in fourth for convincing win to continue domination of Nets

For three quarters, the Knicks were playing down to their opponent. They were, in a word, terrible. Knicks-Nets was so ugly it should've been blurred out for indecency.

But then the defending champs woke up. OG Anunoby caught fire, Josh Hart provided a spark, Jordan Clarkson took advantage, and the Knicks ultimately cakewalked to a 103-90 victory Monday at MSG.

"We finally played fast, got stops," said Hart, who had eight points and seven assists in 27 minutes. "We've been playing very slow, very stagnant. We finally got out, we ran and we got good looks."

New York Knicks guard Jalen Brunson (11) reacts after hitting a 3-point shot during the first half against the Brooklyn Nets, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in New York. Jason Szenes for NY Post

With Jalen Brunson on the bench at the start of the final period, the Knicks (3-4) went from a four-point deficit to a 10-point advantage in about four minutes. It spurred the Knicks' 15th straight victory over the Nets (1-6), a streak that began in 2023 — right after the Durant-Kyrie era in Brooklyn.

But Monday was in doubt for the majority of the night.

"The issues are still there," Hart said. "I think defensively, we were solid. I think this year, for the most part, our defense has been pretty good. Obviously there have been some lapses. … But our offense has to get in a better flow, and that's kind of what's holding us back right now. So a win doesn't erase the problem or erase the issues. We got to figure those out and get better."

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle (30) drives to the basket against New York Knicks guard Mikal Bridges (25) on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in New York. Jason Szenes for NY Post

The first half was a slopfest from both sides, an absolute eyesore. The Knicks shot 36 percent but still held a seven-point advantage at the break because of their abundance of free throws.

Brunson and Landry Shamet were the lone bright spots. The Nets were a misfiring mess but found a rhythm after the break — particularly Michael Porter Jr. — and won the third quarter 35-24.

Mike Brown responded with a new lineup — with Tyler Kolek and Clarkson in the backcourt — and the Nets folded with just 11 total points in the fourth quarter.

Anunoby scored eight of his 18 points in the first six minutes of the fourth quarter. Karl-Anthony Towns finished with 16 rebounds and 11 points. Brunson had 19 points but was again inefficient on 6-for-16 shooting. Shamet scored all 20 of his points in the first three quarters. Clarkson took over in the fourth. Mikal Bridges, meanwhile, pulled another Houdini and was benched for all but three minutes of the second half. He had six points on 1-for-7 shooting from the field.

New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns (32) pulls down a rebound against Brooklyn Nets forward Chaney Johnson (31) on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in New York. Jason Szenes for NY Post

For the Nets, Julius Randle, the former Knicks All-Star, forced the issue and often missed — shooting 5-for-16 with a team-high 21 points.

The interborough matchup, once touted as a powerful rivalry when the Nets moved from New Jersey to Brooklyn, now carries the juice of a steamrolled grape. The Knicks have always dominated the attention of New York City, and it's never been more lopsided than in 2026 — the year of James Dolan's first title.

The Nets have become an afterthought under the stewardship of GM Sean Marks, who is second to only Brian Cashman as the longest-tenured top sports executive in town. They haven't won a playoff game since 2021, and there's little hope Marks' latest incarnation will break the streak.

They have two win-now veterans in Randle and Porter, who are surrounded by the young and unproven, operating a great distance from contending basketball.

Still, the Knicks were in trouble and playing ugly ball until the fourth quarter began. They're still not where they want to be.

"We definitely played until June last season and I think everyone still feels it," Anunoby said.

#2986. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2984. Anthropic reports $17 billion in Q3 revenue, purchase commitments of $895.4 billion in revised S-1

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.

The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, though most of that was due to the change in fair value of convertible notes and derivative liabilities. Anthropic had a loss of $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.

Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic initial public offering. The initial filing only included the Claude maker's financials through the first half of the year.

During its third quarter, Anthropic generated roughly $17 billion in revenue, up about 1,127% from the same period last year. Revenue growth slowed on a quarter-to-quarter basis, however. Third-quarter revenue increased around 47%, a steep drop from 144% growth in Q2 and 132% in Q1.

The company reported a net loss of $23.29 billion during its third quarter.

As the 5-year-old company prepares to kick off its roadshow, it will have to reckon with growing market jitters around the AI buildout. Wall Street has been worried that rising interest rates will make debt unaffordable for many data center developers and neoclouds, and quarterly reports from Microsoft, Alphabet and Amazon last week added fuel to those concerns.

The hyperscalers' aggressive AI spending is eating into their cash flows, causing some investors to worry that computing capacity may not come online quickly enough to meet Anthropic's demands. Anthropic gets much of its capacity from Amazon, Google and SpaceX, but it also relies on neoclouds for some compute as it races to expand as quickly as possible.

In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

Anthropic said its compute and infrastructure expenses increased by $14.4 billion for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up 329% compared with the same period last year.

"As our compute and infrastructure capacity grows, the associated compute and infrastructure expenses have increased," Anthropic said in the filing.

The company's CEO, Dario Amodei, co-founded Anthropic alongside a handful of other researchers who defected from OpenAI, now its chief rival. OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the SEC in June, but its executives have signaled that it's aiming for a 2027 debut.

Anthropic has worked to position itself as more safety-conscious than its competitors, and its S-1 includes more than 90 pages of risk factors. The company highlights risks, including potential political headwinds, its complex infrastructure needs as well as the difficulties that can come from balancing safety and commercial objectives.

"We consider it our personal responsibility to help the world understand these risks, to prevent or mitigate them when they arise from our AI models, and to work with governments and the rest of the industry to address them at a societal level," Anthropic's co-founders said in the filing.

#2980. Player Grades: Cavs vs Thunder – Donovan Mitchell dishes 11 assists in loss

The Cleveland Cavaliers missed an opportunity against an injury-riddled Oklahoma City Thunder in their 117-112 loss.

Grades are based on our usual expectations for each player.

16 points, 7 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 blocks, 2 steals

Kenny Atkinson said before the game that OKC's defense would swarm the paint and make it difficult to feed the Cleveland bigs. He was right, as Mobley was forced out of the paint for the first three quarters and took roughly half of his shots from the perimeter.

It wasn't until the fourth that Mobley was able to consistently assert himself in the paint. The Cavs found him on the roll multiple times, as he either converted at the rim or earned trips to the free-throw line. It wasn't enough, but it was a quality adjustment that's worth noting.

17 points, 11 assists, 5 rebounds, 3 turnovers

Mitchell struggled with his shot but worked his way to 11 assists by zipping the ball around the floor and being a willing passer. He looked particularly spry in the first quarter, wheeling and dealing without the threat of OKC's usual perimeter defense.

The added playmaking was great and deserves to be mentioned. But, at the end of the day, the Cavs needed more than just 17 points on 17 shots.

14 points, 6 assists, 4 rebounds, 1 turnover

Harden has settled into a groove in recent games. His turnovers are down, and his efficiency is on the rise. He shot 5-for-10 from the floor and generated advantages for his teammates throughout the night.

However, Harden's off-ball defense continues to be a weak point for Cleveland. And, the Cavs would like to see him score at a higher rate, especially when most of the team is struggling to shoot from downtown.

Thomas started tonight for Peyton Watson, marking the rookie's first-ever start. He was beat on a handful of backdoor cuts, but held his own in isolation and was a positive influence on Cleveland's offense. Thomas never stops running around the perimeter, and that activity, when packaged with his three-point shooting, helps to grease the wheels of any given possession.

I have to knock Thomas half a grade for smoking an open layup in crunch time. Sorry, Rook.

14 points, 6 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 steal, 1 block

Allen had one of the best blocks he'll have all season when he rejected Aday Mara at the rim to end the third quarter.

Other than that, Allen faced issues similar to Mobley. The Thunder defense loaded up in the paint and played drop coverage throughout the night. This made it hard to keep the bigs involved, but — Allen did take and make an elbow jumper in the fourth quarter to help alleviate this.

Allen was not featured in Cleveland's closing lineup. A decision that continues to feel self-defeating, especially when the team is looking for key stops on the defensive end.

12 points, 8 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 steals

The Cavs are still working out the best way to use Hezonja, but tonight might be the blueprint they've been looking for.

Hezonja was immensely helpful off the bench, grabbing boards and smacking three-pointers. His tanky build makes him a unique role player who can do a little bit of everything. That fluidity has made it hard for Cleveland to define. But maybe, that's a strength.

Merrill is going through a cold spell to start the season as he shot 2-for-8 from downtown tonight and missed a chance to tie the game with under a minute to play.

Proctor finally looked like the player we expected to see this season. His point-of-attack defense helped keep the Thunder from running away with a lead during the first half, and his three-point shooting was just as helpful.

This is a player who should plug-and-play in any lineup the Cavs run. That makes him a versatile 3-and-D guard who can deliver timely performances in a pinch like tonight.

#2977. Supreme Court rejects DOJ's bid to block California from counting disputed ballots

The Supreme Court, in an order Monday evening, denied the Department of Justice's last-minute request to temporarily block California from counting mail-in ballots from about 20,000 voters who it suggested may not be U.S. citizens.

The one-sentence order came hours after the DOJ sought the injunction, and on the eve of Election Day.

The Supreme Court did not explain its reason for the denial, and it did not note any dissents. The order came after Justice Elena Kagan presented the DOJ's request to the full court, which includes three appointees of President Donald Trump.

The swift ruling was the latest courtroom blow to the Trump administration, which is engaged in multiple other legal fights related to the midterm elections.

Those contests will determine which political party holds majorities in the House and Senate. Pollsters and analysts widely predict Democrats will make significant gains.

On Sunday, a federal judge dismissed a DOJ lawsuit that sought to block Alaska election officials from immediately certifying the result of the U.S. Senate election there if incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan loses that race by a margin that could be attributable to voter confusion about another candidate with his name.

California Attorney General Rob Bonta told CNBC that Monday's denial by the Supreme Court was "completely appropriate, very expected."

"This was a Hail Mary. It was baseless," Bonta said in a phone interview. "And the U.S. Supreme Court's response showed just how baseless it was."

The attorney general noted both the speed of the Supreme Court's order and the fact that it was issued without the court first asking California to respond to the DOJ's request.

"That is very rare," Bonta said, adding that, "We haven't seen this type of order where they didn't even ask for a response since 2018."

The DOJ's application to the Supreme Court came three days after the 9th Circuit U.S. Court of Appeals rejected the department's attempt to overturn a federal district court judge's ruling that allowed the California ballots to be counted.

That same day, another federal district judge in a separate case in California barred DOJ from threatening election workers with criminal prosecution for refusing to stop processing ballots or resisting other demands.

During a hearing Monday in that second case, a DOJ lawyer admitted to Judge Trina Thompson that the lists of purported noncitizens on California's voter rolls had a "high error rate," MS NOW reported. But that lawyer also argued the errors are evidence of why election officials need to examine those rolls and make sure that everyone on them is a U.S. citizen.

Solicitor General D. John Sauer, in his application Monday morning asking the Supreme Court to enjoin counting of the mail ballots, said, "In this case, this Office has been informed that the United States has identified more than 20,000 individuals who appear to be registered to vote in California based on publicly available information at the time that information was received, but who, in the United States' view, are likely not U.S. citizens."

Sauer said "the United States is likely to succeed on the merits of its appeal" of the lower court ruling denying the DOJ's request.

"The district court concluded that the United States lacks Article III standing to bring this suit, but the United States suffers a judicially cognizable 'injury to its sovereignty' from the 'violation of its laws,'" Sauer said in the petition.

"Once California commingles the challenged ballots with lawful ones, there is no way to get the challenged ballots back."

The DOJ sued California on Oct. 15 in U.S. District Court in Los Angeles. The complaint sought to prevent state election officials from tabulating mail-in ballots "from likely non-citizens in the upcoming federal election until the voter's citizenship status can be determined."

The DOJ previously gave Los Angeles County officials a thumb drive containing names of voters the department suggested could be noncitizens, who by law are barred from voting in federal elections.

Last Wednesday, Judge David Carter denied the DOJ's request that he order California officials to set aside mail ballots from alleged noncitizens named on a list prepared by the Department of Homeland Security, and not count their votes until their citizenship is confirmed.

"This case is about the right of every American citizen to vote in a free and fair election," Carter said in his ruling.

"Never before in American history has the federal government, on the eve of an election, sought to coerce a state and local government to abandon their own Constitutional elections procedures and bend to the will of the Executive Branch," Carter wrote.

"In bringing this lawsuit, the Executive would have this Court believe that it reigns as a sole sovereign. But California and its people are sovereign, too."

The DOJ then appealed that ruling to the 9th Circuit. A three-judge panel on that court denied the appeal in a 2-1 ruling on Friday

"There is a material risk that granting the United States its requested relief would result in disenfranchisement," said the ruling by the majority, Circuit Judges Mark Bennett and Gabriel Sanchez. Bennett was appointed by Trump, and Sanchez was appointed by former President Joe Biden.

#2974. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2972. Hawks escape six-man Pacers 108–104 after record-tying first half

The Atlanta Hawks escaped with a 108–104 victory over an Indiana Pacers team with just six available players Monday, surviving a late rally behind Nickeil Alexander-Walker's 34 points.

Alexander-Walker connected on nine of his 15 three-point attempts. CJ McCollum finished one rebound shy of a triple-double with 13 points, 12 assists and nine rebounds, while Onyeka Okongwu contributed 13 points and 10 rebounds.

Atlanta made 15 three-pointers before halftime, tying the franchise record for any half. However, its hot shooting gave way to a stagnant offense after the break, allowing the depleted Pacers to fight their way back.

The Hawks were also missing key players, with Jalen Johnson sidelined by a left ankle sprain and Kingston Flemings serving a one-game suspension. Still, against an Indiana team operating with such limited personnel, Atlanta made the finish considerably harder than it needed to be.

The opening quarter offered little indication of those later struggles. Atlanta shot 60 percent from the field and 70 percent from three-point range, establishing an early offensive rhythm.

Rookie Zuby Ejiofor also made his regular-season debut, connecting from beyond the arc for his first NBA basket.

The Hawks maintained control through the second quarter, although Indiana showed the persistence that kept it competitive throughout the night. An 11–2 Pacers run cut the deficit to single digits, but Atlanta continued making perimeter shots to finish its record-tying half.

After halftime, those shots stopped falling as consistently. Atlanta led by as many as 15 points, but Indiana put together a 7–0 third-quarter run to help trim the deficit to five.

The Hawks' ball movement stalled, leaving them with stagnant possessions against a more active Pacers defense. Indiana outscored Atlanta 23–19 in the quarter, setting up a competitive finish.

Atlanta continued leaving the door open in the fourth. Fouls gave the Pacers opportunities at the free-throw line, while a late 9–0 Indiana run made it a one-possession game.

The Hawks ultimately held on, but the result did little to erase concerns about their second-half execution. Alexander-Walker's shooting and McCollum's playmaking helped secure the victory, yet Atlanta struggled to sustain the offensive flow needed to put away its opponent.

Indiana deserves credit for its effort. Despite having only six players available, the Pacers continued competing, increased their defensive pressure and gave themselves a chance to win late.

For Atlanta, the victory was welcome, but the performance left plenty to address. A record-tying shooting half should have provided the foundation for a more controlled finish. Instead, the Hawks needed to survive a final push from a severely shorthanded opponent.

The Hawks next face the Washington Wizards on Wednesday, hoping to have Jalen Johnson and Jock Landale back in the lineup.

#2970. OpenAI hires former U.S. Army Secretary Dan Driscoll for national security role

OpenAI has hired former U.S. Army Secretary Dan Driscoll as senior counselor for national security modernization, a newly created role at the company.

Driscoll will focus on how artificial intelligence can be used to strengthen U.S. national security and cybersecurity, OpenAI said, while his updated LinkedIn profile also lists his new position.

He will work with OpenAI's senior leadership and external partners, with a particular focus on helping U.S. allies adopt advanced AI responsibly.

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said the U.S. has "an extraordinary opportunity to work with other countries to spread the adoption of democratic AI," and that Driscoll's appointment would help strengthen American leadership in the technology.

"National security requirements are changing faster than at any time in the past fifty years," Driscoll said. He added that AI could help reduce paperwork, process information and deliver answers more quickly to those working in national security.

The appointment comes after Driscoll stepped down as Army secretary earlier this year after months of reported tensions with Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth.

An Iraq war veteran, Driscoll pushed efforts to modernize the U.S. military, including expanding access to drone and counter-drone technologies among U.S. allies, according to a previous U.S. Army announcement.

Driscoll also met with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy in Kyiv in November 2025 to discuss a U.S.-proposed peace plan aimed at ending the war with Russia, according to a statement from the Presidential Office of Ukraine.

At OpenAI, Driscoll will work with government officials, industry leaders, academic institutions and allied organizations to advance what the company calls "democratic AI." He will also help internal teams understand the operational needs of national security institutions, building on OpenAI's efforts to strengthen cyber resilience and protect essential services through its Daybreak program.

#2967. Feeling isolated? Half of in-office workers describe their desk jobs as 'remote,' NY Fed survey finds

Many workers across the U.S. are now back in-office a majority of the time, and they are feeling more isolated than ever.

A recent survey from researchers at the New York Fed finds that roughly half of workers based in-office don't describe a daily experience on the job that matches the goal many corporate employers stated in upping in-person mandates.

Whether three-, four- or five-day mandates, companies have cited the importance of creative collaboration for business productivity and growth, as well as mentorship opportunities. Yet workers surveyed by the NY Fed describe endless Zoom calls and Slacks, and little actual face time.

"Days in the office often lack human contact," the researchers write. Specifically, 48.8% of survey respondents said they had no in-person meetings, either with teammates or non-teammates, on the days they were most recently in the office.

There are a variety of reasons for the disconnect between in-office work mandates and actual experience.

Roughly one in four workers are now fully remote, according to U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics data cited by the NY Fed, but they say that alone likely "understates the explosion in the experience of remoteness from colleagues."

With teams being spread across more offices — an effect they say has become more pronounced in the post-Covid organizational structure — many workers "no-showing" on mandates, and employers not enforcing mandates strictly, the result for in-office workers, according to the researchers, is that "they commute in just to take Zoom calls and answer Slack messages from their cubicles."

The NY Fed paper, by NY Fed research economist Natalia Emanuel and University of Virginia economics professor Emma Harrington, concludes that "a staggering share of the workforce experiences this form of in-office remote work."

The survey was conducted in September among a final sample of 1,835 college-educated workers. The researchers chose September, they say, to generate a "conservative estimate," since many companies push in-office attendance after summer ends, and the period between Labor Day and Thanksgiving has less remote work than holiday periods.

The survey found that on in-office days, 70.6% of workers had at least one teammate who was not in the office; while half had no in-person meetings with all participants present, and over a third had neither in-person or hybrid meetings.

"Amazingly, 37% of respondents reported that they had at least one meeting that was virtual, even though all the participants were in the same office that day. In short, many workers' commutes are simply yielding a new background for their Zoom calls," they write.

They cite the example of Zoom CEO Eric Yuan, who said in 2023, "On Zoom, it's really hard. We cannot have a great conversation. We cannot debate each other well."

But the researchers conclude, "if the debates in the office are also happening over Zoom, the benefits of the office may not be so large."

The authors say that separate information collected on a Fortune 500 company employee base to which they had personnel data and badge swipe data shows that nearly half of corporate employees have at least one teammate who is out of the office on their in-office days. They say additional data from the BLS's American Time Use Survey similarly show that "Americans now spend more time physically alone on days they go into the office than they did before the pandemic."

Other recent polling data from Gallup found worker sentiment to be at a 15-year low.

The NY Fed authors caution that their research does not capture relationships with non-teammates, or worker's in-person interactions with clients, often outside the office. They also stress that workers who come to the office often cite seeing colleagues as a major draw. "But since the rise of remote work, the office increasingly fails to deliver this benefit," they write.

They contend that the research has practical implications for both employers and employees.

For one, the experience of remote work is much more widespread than understood, which could have many repercussions not being dealt with effectively now, such as mental health issues.

Second, organizational structure post-pandemic may not be optimal for making in-office mandates work. It may, in fact, just lead to more pushback, they write.

But the authors do not conclude employers should give up on the mandates. To the contrary, they write, "ensuring adherence to the policy helps deliver the face-to-face contact that workers crave."

#2966. 3 takeaways as the Mavericks fall into a 115-100 trap against the Sacramento Kings

The Dallas Mavericks (4-3) fell to the Sacramento Kings (1-6), 115-100, in a Monday night matchup at the American Airlines Center. The game against the previously winless Kings followed the Mavericks' Friday night win in Game 1 of NBA Cup group play. Dallas was unable to overcome night in the freezer on offense against a Kings team that was desperately seeking its first win on Monday.

Kyrie Irving was ruled out early Monday evening due to left knee injury management. Santi Aldama, Tarik Biberovic, Sergio de Larrea, and Dereck Lively were also out for the Mavericks. Keegan Murray, Maxime Raynaud, and Dylan Cardwell were the notable players absent from the Kings.

Walter Clayton Jr. and Daniel Gafford led the Mavericks with 20 points apiece. Darius Acuff led all scorers with 25 points for the Kings.

Mavericks head coach Dusty May decided to go with a no-guard starting lineup, inserting Caleb Martin into the first five with Irving's absence. The game started slowly for both teams, with both squads struggling to get their offense going in the first quarter. Dallas played 10 of the first 12 minutes of the first quarter without a point guard in the lineup, and the offense struggled as a result. The Mavericks finished the first period down 19-18 in a game that was quickly devolving into a snooze fest.

The Mavericks entered the second quarter more inspired, finally getting shots to fall behind Daniel Gafford shots at the rim. Once Cooper Flagg got going, the Mavericks gave themselves a little breathing room from the Kings. Walter Clayton gave Dallas the offensive boost it needed in the second period, but the Mavericks were unable to string together enough stops to separate themselves from the Kings.

Morez Johnson Jr. was a bright spot in this quarter for the Mavericks, playing aggressively on the glass and imposing his strength, getting to the foul line on three occasions. Sacramento punched back behind a string of buckets from Malik Monk, and the Mavericks walked into the intermission with the same deficit they faced after the first quarter, down 54-53.

Clayton started the second half for the Mavericks after it was announced that Max Christie would not be returning to the game due to a hip injury. Dallas entered the third quarter as slowly as they played the first half, struggling to get the offense going in any capacity. The Mavericks continued to try to claw their way out of the hole they found themselves in, but entered the final quarter down 81-74.

Dallas continued to struggle throughout the fourth quarter, with Clayton being the only Maverick to see shots fall late. Darius Acuff and Malik Monk continued to take advantage of the Mavericks' defensive missteps, completely shifting the momentum in the Kings' favor. The Mavericks were ultimately unable to dig themselves out of the deficit they faced all game, falling 115-100.

Here are three takeaways from tonight's loss.

When it was announced that Irving would be out for tonight's matchup, concerns arose regarding how the Mavericks offense would operate in his absence. Naji Marshall struggled in his return tonight after missing the last three games due to an illness. Christie continued to make no impact before leaving the game with that hip injury. The Mavs tried to shoot themselves out of their offensive struggles, to no success, finishing 7-of-33 from behind the arc.

The Mavericks are going to need more from guys like Marshall and PJ Washington when Irving is out, or they will continue to struggle in games where Dallas decides to rest Irving. Clayton stepped up when the Mavericks needed offensive contributions, but the Mavericks cannot depend on a two-way player every night.

"Shots weren't falling, and it kind of got in our head," Gafford said in a televised portion of his postgame press conference. "They wanted it more than we did. The energy was a little low."

Morez Johnson Jr. set a career-high in points tonight, with 13 points, and added two rebounds. Johnson was a bright spot in tonight's game, holding his own against Domantas Sabonis and Precious Achiuwa. He played physically, grabbing two offensive rebounds to lead to extra possessions for the Mavericks. He was also able to exert his strength to get to the foul line, though he shot 3-of-6 from the charity stripe. The ninth pick in the 2026 NBA Draft played more comfortably tonight and kept his foul count low again tonight, a pain point for him in the first several games for the Mavericks. Coach May gave insight into how he has coached Johnson before tonight's game.

Johnson highlighted this tonight, fighting on the glass and playing physically in the paint. Johnson will grow as a player if he continues to do the little things like he did tonight.

Cooper Flagg struggled against the Kings following his 33-point performance against the Rockets on Friday. He finished with 17 points on 3-of-19 from the field. Flagg struggled to take over offensively in Irving's absence. He was able to get to the free-throw line at a high volume, but failed to see his shots fall all night long. Luckily, this will be one of the worst performances we see from him. Dallas was unable to provide any contributions behind Flagg, putting this one out of reach for the Mavericks.

The Mavericks are back in action Wednesday night as they travel to San Antonio to face the Spurs. Catch the action at 8:30 p.m. CT on ESPN.

#2964. Winners and Losers from Seahawks vs. Bears: Is it time to fire Brian Fleury?

No, the Seattle Seahawks don't have a "could've won if…" situation. They got their asses kicked by the Chicago Bears in a 34-17 game that was somehow both not as close and yet closer than the final score indicated. Seattle doesn't get to play the "barely lost" game this time around. This was a thoroughly deserved win for the Bears and a humbling defeat for the Super Bowl champs.

It's late at night and instead of prepping for Enemy Reaction, I'm here writing another disappointing Winners and Losers column on the cusp of midnight.

Jaxon Smith-Njigba is the Seahawks offense

I repeat, Jaxon Smith-Njigba is the Seahawks offense. He has almost 1,000 yards receiving through eight games, and he was honestly cheated out of that because of a couple of "run plays" that were narrowly backward. There isn't a better receiver in the NFL and Seattle is blessed to have him in the now and long-term.

Minus the one asinine penalty, which at that point likely didn't swing the game, I see the vision for Irv as a special teams superstar. He downed a Michael Dickson punt inside the 10 (doing what Rodney Thomas botched), was in on tackles on kick return duties, balling out as a gunner, and he had a deflected punt which unluckily went for 45 yards. He's worth a conditional 7th in 2028.

Zach Charbonnet being back is a personal win for him

Charbonnet had just 23 yards on six carries and it wasn't like the offensive line was opening up too many holes. I think for him, it's an accomplishment to be back on the field inside of 10 months after an ACL tear. That's a lot of rehab and effort to go through to be available when a January injury of that severity can cost you all of next year. I want to highlight that for Charbs.

DeMarcus Lawrence kept Seattle in the game

For the first time all season (unless you count Deebo Samuel dropping the ball because he blew out his hamstring), a Seahawks defender forced a fumble on a non-quarterback. Tank got the FF and the FR. I wish this swan song was going better.

Outside of one deep shot to Jaxon Smith-Njigba for a touchdown, what did Darnold do well? Avoid sacks? No. Throw on time? Not really. Throw accurately on a consistent basis? Nope. The offensive line was bad and certainly Brian Fleury's shelf life as an OC is now under question, but Darnold's statistical downturn can be traced back to the four-interception meltdown against the Los Angeles Rams. With few exceptions (most notably and thankfully the NFC Championship Game), Darnold has leaned toward being an average quarterback at best for about an 18-game stretch. And if you think I'm being unfair lumping the Kubiak season with the Fleury games, he did not end the regular season well in 2025. The run game just got hot at the right time to replace some of the explosives lost in the passing game.

He touched the wrong quadrant in the back-end of 2025 but salvaged things with a great playoff run. He's back to being in the wrong quadrant in 2026.

That was a showing worse than either of the Denver Broncos or San Francisco 49ers losses. We can likely shelve "start Drew Lock" while Lock is nursing his own injury, but at the very least he is not in any contract extension territory. He can play out his contract in 2027 or leave sooner than that if things don't turn positively.

That one-week reprieve where it looked like he found the magical potion sure wore off quickly. Why were the Seahawks taking an age and huddling up down by 10 in the 4th quarter? Why did they call so many run plays to the right side of the line? No urgency, no easy offense, no cohesiveness, no sensible gameplan. Once again they stalled out in the red zone because they burned first down on a doomed running play and took no end zone shot. I'm sure he interviewed well and knows ball extremely well, but there are a lot of great mathematicians who make terrible teachers. Fleury is not a great OC right now and the repeated dysfunction in situational football and in the general flow of the game is reaching untenable status.

Mike Macdonald moved on from Ryan Grubb after one season and he probably would've moved on sooner if he was not a rookie head coach. Fleury may not be here past the bye week with how ruinous the offense is. I can't blame Fleury when guys fumble or when Sam Darnold goes into self-destruct mode unforced, but I can blame him to some degree when the offense as a whole doesn't look well coached.

It's not wholly unexpected when you have a backup left tackle and a left guard who's now a right guard because the new left guard is Quenton Nelson. There was going to be a problem getting everyone organized. This is not a well-functioning unit even when healthy. Abe Lucas continues to struggle and it's more of a concern about his physical health than anything else. Zabel got owned by Jeffery Simmons off the jump, and the Bears regularly got pressure with four. Forget about the run game woes.

This was supposed to be a strength and may yet be a strength when Charles Cross is back and we figure out Jalen Sundell's injury, but right now they're accomplices in the first-degree felony that is this offense.

A week after one of the best games of his career, it was a tough night for Emmanwori in terms of run defense and tackling. Between missed tackles, getting beaten on the touchdown to Luther Burden, and some difficult reps against Colston Loveland, this was easily one of his more disappointing outings. The screen to Kalif Raymond was the dagger and he was blocked out of the play. Ben Johnson made it a point to pick on him and he lacked any positive impact.

And the roughness penalty (again!) he picked up when the game was still reasonably in doubt was selfish and he needs as much criticism for that as we regularly gave Riq Woolen. He's done that twice in less than a month and it's led to points both times.

Rylie Mills' training camp has not translated into positive regular season play

It wasn't like the Seahawks run defense struggled or anything, but Mills entering the game is a signal to the opposition to run at him. He's driven back by double teams too easily, struggles with his anchor, and isn't offering enough as a pass rusher. This leaves Seattle essentially three-deep on the interior with Leonard Williams, Byron Murphy II, and Jarran Reed. I can't imagine justifying more snaps for Mills if Williams has to miss time.

Special teams outside of Irv Charles was not good

In fact, they were mostly bad. Rodney Thomas inexplicably took a punt downed at the 2 and slid himself and the ball into the end zone. The trajectory of that ball was going away from the end zone and the Bears gained 18 yards of field position before scoring a touchdown.

Jason Myers shanked a PAT and later had a short field goal blocked. Mike Morris committed a really silly late hit on a kick return that gifted the Bears terrific field position. Even Irv Charles, who had a great night, sullied it with his own silly roughness penalty.

Mike Macdonald has to own this complete unpreparedness

Coach Macdonald has won a Super Bowl and Seattle has won far more than they've lost in two-and-a-half seasons with him at the helm. This is as bad a three-phase effort as this team has put out. Maybe not since the Buffalo Bills blowout two years ago, or the Green Bay Packers blowout in primetime.

The defense gave up an opening drive touchdown, didn't have a lot of answers for the intermediate play-action game, got caught out on the most pivotal third down of the game because they still can't stop quick screens to receivers, and the poor discipline and losing their heads on personal fouls is a reflection of a coaching staff that does not have its players pulling together as one.

Coach has to own this as much as we would excoriate Pete Carroll for a similar display.

Seattle's playoff hopes are not in peril but they are uncertain

Throw out the NFC East and probably the NFC South because those are poor divisions. Seattle is fighting to finish second in the NFC West and be at least ahead of one NFC North team, or else the NFC North could have everyone make the postseason. This team is not playing sound enough football to be trusted to beat literally any NFL team comfortably—just look at the mess that was the Los Angeles Chargers game—and cannot be favorites to beat the 49ers in a rematch. They need to prove themselves all over again because they've lost four of six and have looked discombobulated on offense and at times on special teams.

Relying on the defense to be superhuman every week is a losing strategy. This is not anywhere close to what we saw in 2025.

Tory Horton got his world rocked to start the game but he's really not impressed with his catch-point strength. I noticed this last year and it showed itself again this year. Seattle may have to have a serious rethink about its WR group outside of JSN next season.

Jadarian Price seemed very hesitant to cut upfield on the few times Seattle actually created space. I'll consider it rust combined with the issues along the OL. At least he didn't fumble.

Byron Murphy II and Leonard Williams provided good interior pressure, but the edges were unsurprisingly unable to do a whole lot.

I think Nehemiah Pritchett should be splitting snaps with Josh Jobe. It was not a great night overall for Jobe in terms of pass coverage and Pritchett has been impressive every week. He may not be an outside corner based on how Seattle has used him, but he has the frame so why not get him some more opportunities. Likewise for Tyrice Knight to get more snaps, because he's made enough strides to get back in the rotation.

It's almost midnight. I'm tapped out. A lot more to write about on Tuesday and throughout the week. If Seattle gets to the bye at 6-4 then at least there's hope. Anything less than that and I think they're cooked, because they don't have a lot of room for error the rest of the way.

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2961. Rockets snap Celtics’ five-game win streak, 109-93

The Houston Rockets hosted the Boston Celtics on a second night of a back-to-back, Monday night. Boston kept things close in the first half, but eventually ran out of gas in Houston. Alperen Sengun led all scorers with 26 points, Jayson Tatum had 19 points and Neemias Queta 16 points for the C's. Boston now move to 5 and 2 on the season, and Houston go clear in the Western Conference with seven wins and 1 loss.

Boston rested veteran Paul George for the night after a nice road win over the Orlando Magic on Sunday. Baylor Scheierman and Hugo Gonzalez got the starting nod alongside Derrick White, Jayson Tatum and Neemias Queta for Boston. The Houston Rockets started the game with Fred VanVleet, Amen Thompson, Kevin Durant, Jabari Smith Jr, and Alperen Sengun.

The full-strength Houston Rockets got off to a good start with five points to start the game. Amen Thompson was aggressive attacking the basket for six quick points. Kevin Durant had a pair of turn-around jumpers to start the game for the home team.

Jayson Tatum was looking to get others involved; fresh off his Eastern Conference player of the week award, he had 2 assists at the six-minute mark. Baylor Scheierman had five points to start the game before he took a seat on the bench.

Mitchell Robinson, Sam Hauser and Payton Pritchard were first off the bench for Boston. Robinson would go to the locker room late in the first quarter with a thumb sprain after only playing a few minutes. Tari Eason hit 2 threes off the bench for Houston as they took an early ten-point lead and looked in control.

Boston missed 32 of 44 3-pointers in Orlando last night, and they continued to struggle shooting the ball to start the game with just 1-9 from downtown. Luka Garza got into the game and had a big impact on the scoreboard, hitting two straight triples in just three minutes of play. Boston had tied the game after a quarter of play, 29-29.

Mike Conley started the second quarter in his second game of the year for his new team; he hit a three-pointer before going back to the bench in a very short stint of play.

The Houston Rockets outscored the Celtics by seven points in the second quarter as Jayson Tatum got a rest after playing the first 12 minutes of the game. Alperen Sengun paced the Rockets with twelve points in the half on 6 of 8 shooting from the field, doing most of his damage in the paint.

Luka Garza hit his third 3-pointer of the game from the corner on a wide open attempt, he had 9 points at the half in just eight minutes of play. Mitchell Robinson returned to the court halfway through the second quarter with a strapped hand. The big man peeled off a screen to dunk home a two-hander off a beautiful Jayson Tatum lob pass.

Payton Pritchard was scoreless for 14 minutes of the game before he finally scored from the foul line. The Celtics had 6 turnovers at the half; shooting just 42% from the field and going just 7 of 22 from three. Jayson Tatum led the C's with 11 points, 4 rebounds and 4 assists, all 11 players had scored for Boston at the half.

The Boston Celtics started the second half with a pair of big men, Neemias Queta and Mitchell Robinson against the taller Rockets squad. The lineup switch paid off, as Neemias Queta was rolling for Boston with 7 straight points; he hit a rare corner triple and got a pair of tip-ins to start the quarter.

Jayson Tatum and Mitchell Robinson connected on a second thunderous lob of the night as Boston cut Houston's lead back to one point. Hugo Gonzalez had a breakaway layup attempt blocked by Alperen Sengun on the chase down. Jabari Smith Jr. went down the other end and drilled a three-pointer as Houston went up by 7 points.

Payton Pritchard had a chance to cut Houston's lead to 2 points, but he missed a wide-open three-pointer. The shooting woes continued for Boston; Derrick White, Sam Hauser and Pritchard were a combined 0-11 from three after 3 quarters of play. Houston took a 9-point lead into the fourth quarter, 73-82 at the end of three.

Marcus Smart flopped on two straight plays to start the fourth quarter, Payton Pritchard converted on just 1 of 2 free throws. Marcus Smart hit a pair of triples, Reed Shepard hit his first points of the game as Joe Mazzulla called a timeout to discuss things, Houston were up 93-79.

Derrick White finally connected on his first 3-pointer of the night, after 26 minutes of game time. Boston ran of legs in this one, Houston's swarming defenders taking their toll on the second night of a back-to-back. Chris Cenac Jr. and Dillon Mitchell got into the game for the final two minutes as Joe Mazzulla waved the white flag in H-Town.

Boston have a day off before next hosting the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday at 7:30 pm EST at TD Garden.

#2960. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2958. Aramco's profit rises as refining margins strengthen amid Iran war

Aramco reported a 9% rise in third-quarter adjusted net income on Tuesday on stronger refining margins.

The Saudi oil giant posted adjusted net income of $31.2 billion for the quarter, up from $28.6 billion a year earlier. The figure exceeded the median analyst consensus estimate of $30.66 billion, according to the company.

Aramco's downstream business, which processes crude oil into fuels and other products, posted adjusted earnings before interest and taxes of $10.3 billion, up 234% from a year earlier, driven by stronger refining margins.

"Geopolitical uncertainty and attacks on key energy facilities continued to amplify the global energy supply crisis in the third quarter of 2026, with serious implications for global energy security," Aramco CEO Amin H. Nasser said in a statement.

Nasser said the company's operational flexibility and supply network enabled it to continue delivering energy to customers despite the disruptions. Aramco is also exploring additional overseas storage capacity and alternative export routes to strengthen its global supply network.

The company generated $28.2 billion in free cash flow during the quarter, up from $23.6 billion a year earlier.

Aramco said its board declared a third-quarter base dividend of $21.9 billion, up 3.5% year on year, to be paid in the fourth quarter.

The company also said its major upstream projects remained on track and highlighted its acquisition of a 49% stake in Saudi artificial intelligence company HUMAIN.

– CNBC's Emma Graham contributed to this story.

#2956. Federal Reserve issues FOMC statement

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2955. San Antonio vs. Utah, Final Score: Two impressive Spurs quarters are plenty versus Jazz, 119-97

Behind its dogged dedication on both ends in the first and third periods, San Antonio ran its undefeated streak to five with a 119-97 win over Utah. After conducting a well-played first 12 minutes with balanced contributions, San Antonio held on to that advantage while fending off an extended Jazz run over much of the second quarter. The Spurs then left no doubt after that, as their starters ran the Jazz off the floor in a 35-20 third period.

Dylan Harper (17 points, 8 assists, 7 rebounds, and 2 steals) led the way for San Antonio (5-2) with seven players (and all starters) getting into double figures. Western Conference Player of the Week Victor Wembanyama (12 points, 11 rebounds, 5 assists, and 3 blocks) seemed content to ride shotgun, as Devin Vassell (11 points) carried the offense over many of the team's second quarter minutes, while newly 22-year-old Stephon Castle (16 points, 5 rebounds, and 4 assists) and De'Aaron Fox (12 points and 3 assists in foul-limited minutes) helped their fellow guards outshine their less experienced Jazz counterparts. Keldon Johnson (15 points and 7 rebounds) was forceful and effective off the bench.

Lauri Markkanen (22 points and 8 rebounds) carried the offense in comparison to Utah's (1-6) callow execution throughout the evening. Jaxson Hayes (9 points and 5 rebounds) and Mo Bamba (8 points and 8 rebounds) provided moments of punch before the Jazz succumbed to the superior Spurs.

All five starters shared well in scoring the team's first 14 points, and the Spurs collectively went up ten early on a sluggish Jazz squad. Utah scores came sparingly throughout the opening frame and their two recent rookie selections combined to go 0-for-8. The Spurs' team defense proved adept both at guiding dribble-drives into the corners away from the rim and crowding the airspace around shooters. San Antonio got in its own way by missing a handful of free throws. All of the impressive two-way execution also was dragged out over a series of referee stoppages throughout the first and yet, the Spurs still left it up 11.

Markkanen found his shooting touch to start the second and helped bring Utah's deficit into single digits. Despite their unrelenting effort to catch and surpass San Antonio, the hosts persevered through many tough minutes throughout the quarter. Vassell and Harper's exploits kept the Spurs afloat, while well-traveled veterans Josh Okogie and Mo Bamba bolstered Markkenen's production.
