#2954. Vikings, Chiefs, Cowboys and Seahawks beaten - NFL week eight

The Minnesota Vikings became the last NFL team to lose their unbeaten record, while the Chicago Bears delivered a statement victory as the 2026 season continued with a wild weekend.

There were a number of rare occurrences during week eight while two games were settled in overtime.

The Dallas Cowboys and the Kansas City Chiefs were both beaten after being stunned by late fightbacks while quarterback Joe Burrow was involved in six touchdowns as the Cincinnati Bengals won again.

Otherwise running backs shone on Sunday, especially for the Tampa Bay Buccaneers as they managed to claim victory without completing a single pass.

And the Baltimore Ravens lost not one but two quarterbacks during their defeat by the Buffalo Bills, including the league's two-time Most Valuable Player, Lamar Jackson.

On Monday Night Football, the Bears defeated the reigning Super Bowl champions the Seattle Seahawks.

Here are the top stories from week eight's action in the NFL.

The Chicago Bears travelled to Seattle and delivered an impressive 34-17 victory over the Seahawks.

D'Andre Swift rushed for three touchdowns as the Bears backed up their previous victory over the New England Patriots - the Super Bowl runners-up.

Swift, 27, scored all three of his touchdowns in the second half, with the Bears' defence, led by newly signed defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons, expertly shutting down the hosts.

It was a third successive win for Chicago, who improve to 6-2 for the season.

Lamar Jackson sustained his latest injury when his hand struck an opponent after throwing the ball [Getty Images]

Two former MVPs went head-to-head as Lamar Jackson returned from three games out with an ankle injury to face Josh Allen's Buffalo Bills.

But that match-up was ruined within moments as Jackson injured his right thumb on the game's first snap, and his back-up Tyler Huntley was then ruled out in the second quarter with a toe injury.

That meant they had to finish the game with undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano at quarterback and Buffalo ran out comfortable 36-10 winners.

The Ravens have lost their past three games and now have a 4-4 record, and Jackson could miss the rest of the season as he is set to undergo surgery.

This is the latest injury blow for Jackson. Although he is one of the most gifted players of his generation, the 29-year-old seems destined not to reach the Super Bowl during his career and the Ravens could miss out on the play-offs for a second straight year.

Huntley could also be out for weeks so the Ravens will have to act fast to bring in an experienced QB or Fagnano will have to start against the Jacksonville Jaguars on Thursday.

NFL's Campbell taken to hospital with neck injury

Minnesota put their 6-0 record on the line at their NFC North rivals the Detroit Lions, who won 19-15 to halt the Vikings' unbeaten start.

Jahmyr Gibbs ran for 153 yards and a touchdown as Detroit (5-2) prevailed in one of several key divisional match-ups.

Lions linebacker Jack Campbell was taken to hospital to be treated for a neck injury suffered while tackling tight end TJ Hockenson in the fourth quarter.

The Denver Broncos (6-2) replaced the Kansas City Chiefs (5-2) at the top of the AFC West after coming from 11 points down in the final quarter to win 28-24.

Patrick Mahomes threw for three touchdowns and 321 yards but that was not enough for the Chiefs as the Broncos' defence made a goal-line stand at the death to clinch victory.

The Jacksonville Jaguars (5-2) are in charge of the AFC South after romping to a 42-7 win over the Indianapolis Colts, who look set for a rebuild.

After firing their general manager and trading two key players in the past two weeks, the Colts are reportedly in talks over trading star running back Jonathan Taylor.

Meanwhile, the Pittsburgh Steelers (6-2) beat the Cleveland Browns 26-7 to go top of the AFC North.

Another of Pittsburgh's divisional rivals, the Cincinnati Bengals, made hard work of it before beating the Tennessee Titans 43-36 in overtime.

Despite being intercepted three times by Tennessee's Amani Hooker, Burrow became the first player in 75 years - and only the second ever after Norm van Brocklin's 554 passing yards in a 1951 game - to record more than 450 passing yards, five touchdown passes and a rushing touchdown in a single game.

After a mixed start to the season, the former first overall draft pick has also become the first quarterback in NFL history to register a 70% completion percentage plus touchdown passes in three consecutive games.

Burrow and his high-powered offence are in ominous form as they seek a Super Bowl return and host Pittsburgh in two weeks.

The Tampa Bay Buccaneers ran the ball 56 times while Atlanta had 28 carries for 99 yards [Getty Images]

While Cincinnati's offence relies on its passing game and receivers, many of the NFL's running backs grabbed the spotlight on Sunday.

For the first time since 2003, at least six players had 140 rushing yards on the same day, including Bucky Irving (170) and Kenneth Gainwell (140) as the Tampa Bay Buccaneers beat the Atlanta Falcons 16-0.

Torrential rain in Florida made handling conditions difficult so Tampa Bay quarterback Baker Mayfield attempted just three passes, completing none of them.

Instead he spent most of the day handing the ball to Irving or Gainwell as the Bucs racked up a team-record 324 rushing yards.

They also became the first team to have zero pass completions since 1974 – and just the second in the Super Bowl era.

Tyler Allgeier was the running back who starred for the Arizona Cardinals, rushing for 147 yards at the Dallas Cowboys.

But it was eccentric back-up quarterback Gardner Minshew who helped turn the game in Arizona's favour after replacing an ineffective Jacoby Brissett with the Cardinals trailing 17-3 late in the third quarter.

After being a late draft pick by Jacksonville in 2019, Minshew has spent most of his career as a back-up and in the off-season Arizona became his sixth team in eight years.

He made a thrilling debut, though, becoming the first quarterback this season to throw three touchdown passes in a fourth quarter.

That tied the game at the death, and although recent hero Chad Ryland failed to kick the extra point, he redeemed himself in overtime with a field goal to win 33-30.

It is only the second time Dallas have ever lost when leading by 10 points inside the final three minutes and leaves them 3-5.

It also heaps pressure on head coach Brian Schottenheimer, with outspoken team owner Jerry Jones saying: "It's an indictment of the whole show".

#2950. Australia hikes rates to 18-year high to fight inflation

Australia's central bank raised its benchmark cash rate by 25 basis points to 4.85% on Tuesday, the highest level since November 2008.

The Reserve Bank of Australia's move was in line with expectations from economists polled by Reuters, and marked its fifth rate increase this year.

In its statement, the RBA said that the move was a split decision, with six members voting for the increase and three opting for a hold.

This also comes as the country continues to grapple with high inflation, with the consumer price index rising 4.3% in September, up from 4% in August and marking its highest level since March.

The central bank also said inflation is now expected to "remain elevated through much of 2027," and not expected to fall back into its 2%-3% target range till late 2027, three quarters later than previously forecast.

This was due to higher fuel and other commodity prices, in addition to factors including AI-related investment, which is driving rapid growth in prices for technology-related goods, the RBA said.

In its September decision, RBA governor Michelle Bullock said that inflation is "too high," driven by higher oil prices from the Iran War and stronger domestic spending.

"We're seeing signs that upside risks to inflation are materializing," she added, saying that additional hikes will come "if that's what's needed to get inflation down."

Growth has slowed in the first two quarters of 2026. Australia's GDP rose 2.1% in the June quarter compared to a year earlier, down from 2.5% in the March quarter.

The RBA is trying to bring inflation back under control while higher borrowing costs and cost-of-living pressures weigh on households and activity.

When asked if she sees a recession for Australia, Bullock said that it is "not our base case at the moment."

#2948. 'I'm happy he's on our side': Bears' Jeffery Simmons looks like perfect fit and Caleb Williams delivers late to humble Seahawks

SEATTLE — The Chicago Bears and Seattle Seahawks on Monday Night Football promised some prime-time drama. What followed was a surprisingly dominant 34-17 Bears win, a victory that exposed the cracks in the reigning Super Bowl champions and the normally impenetrable home environment at Lumen Field.

The Bears entered the game knowing that it would be a tough test. Chicago head coach Ben Johnson said he had "a tremendous amount of respect" for the Seahawks and quarterback Caleb Williams called Seattle a "worthy opponent."

Yet, it was one of the most dominant performances from the Bears all season, on par or a couple of strokes short of the jaw-dropping 59 points they scored in Week 1 at the Carolina Panthers.

Chicago scored 34 points against one of the best defenses in the league. The Bears' defense picked off the Seahawks' Sam Darnold and sacked him three times, and held Seattle to 1-for-11 on third downs. The Bears' special teams blocked a Jason Myers field-goal attempt in the fourth quarter and hit two key field goals of their own to stay ahead in the second quarter. Everything clicked, in all three phases of the field.

"How detailed we are, it's really cool to see those things come to life in big moments and just throughout the game," Williams said.

Part of that came as a result of the instant impact of defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons, who was traded from the Tennessee Titans on Thursday. After a turnaround of three days, Simmons stepped into the Bears' defensive line like he'd always been there, getting a sack (on Seattle's fourth play from scrimmage), six tackles and a pass deflection.

Simmons said he was trying to stay in the moment, even as he went through the whirlwind of a near midseason trade. He traded his signature red sleeves — which he's said he wore so his 3-year-old daughter could recognize him on the field — for orange, and quickly tried to assimilate to a new life with Chicago.

"When I ran out of that tunnel, I just kept telling myself it's, it's just football. Go out there, be me, play by play, be in the moment," Simmons said. "It was a lot of emotion, and I'm grateful to be here and today was a great start."

That great start on defense included Cam Lewis' punishing — and legal — hit of Seahawks wideout Tory Horton on Seattle's first drive that ended in a three-and-out.

Williams and his offense followed by setting the tone of their own with a magnificent opening drive: 17 plays for 80 yards, shaving 8 1/2 minutes off the clock before finishing it off with a 1-yard Luther Burden III touchdown catch.

"That first drive, I thought our guys came out with their hair on fire and I think it carried over for 60 minutes," Johnson said in a postgame news conference. "There were some ebbs and some flows throughout that game, and yet it felt like we responded really well as a team and rallied together."

Simmons got to the Bears' facility late Thursday night, and immediately got to work, knowing he suddenly had to learn a whole new game plan.

"I was getting ready for the Cincinnati Bengals and I had to flip the chapter to get ready for the Seahawks. So it's all about being a pro," Simmons said. "I showed my coaches that this week that I'm prepared and ready to play, and they trusted me to go out there and make sure I was ready."

Williams was impressed by what he saw from Simmons while on the sideline.

"I'm happy he's on our side," Williams said. "He helps everybody on that field on the defense. He helps the D-line when he gets double team[ed]. He frees up other guys to be able to go be special, and then when he gets one-on-ones, [it] allows him to wreak havoc. And so, I'm excited he's on our team. He's a big key piece for us."

Simmons' readiness was clear to Johnson, who said Simmons "fit right in." That early dominance earned Simmons a game ball.

The Bears had plenty of things they prepared for against the Seahawks, including Lumen Field's famous crowd noise and the Seahawks' dominant defensive scheme. Johnson said the team had "one of the more elaborate game plans" for Williams to take on.

"It was a fun week," Williams said with a smirk.

Beyond juggling plays, Williams' confidence and composure held the Bears together on Monday night. After a lost fumble from tight end Colston Loveland — really the only significant mistake from Chicago's offense — led to a Seahawks touchdown, Williams spearheaded a response that led the Bears back into the end zone.

Williams went 24-for-34 passing for 247 yards and a touchdown, and took only one sack.

Williams said that he had told his teammates after the Bears' loss to the Green Bay Packers (a humbling 31-7 defeat while Williams was out with a hamstring injury) that the way they responded was crucial to turning things around.

"When an event happens … the reaction is the most important thing, and that equals the outcome. And so, being able to react a certain way, being able to respond in a certain way, the way we did, is really important for us," Williams said. "We obviously don't want those things to happen, especially in those moments, but when [we] do, let's respond. Let's stay steady and be who we are."

Williams admitted that he didn't have a flawless game Monday. One mistake, a delay of game penalty on the way to the team's second-to-last touchdown, actually bailed him out after he'd misinterpreted a play-call.

"Luckily we got the delay of game, because I ended up messing up a little bit, and it allowed us a little bit more time to be able to fix it," Williams said. "The crowd was loud at that point and I missed a word, one word or so, and that's what happens."

Yet, the Bears kept going. Kalif Raymond rushed for a 39-yard gain as they repeated the third down, and D'Andre Swift finished things off with a touchdown that snuffed out the last of Seattle's comeback spark. It was Swift's second of three TDs.

"We didn't come here to make a statement," Johnson said of the Bears' victory. "We're still building, and this is part of that process right now and, and we've got a lot of really good pieces."

Though Johnson may not want to publicly admit it, this is a win the Bears can build on. Their 6-2 record is the team's best start since 2012; they've beaten tough and not-so-tough teams alike, without letting up on the gas.

And two of those "good pieces" — Williams and Simmons — shined.

#2945. Intel foundry push faces critical test after stock's 45% plunge in October

As Intel comes off its steepest stock drop since the 1970s due to a worsening outlook for the PC market, the chipmaker is facing intensifying pressure to get its flailing foundry business off the ground.

It's been an elusive task for Intel. The company has spent half a decade, and a good chunk of its $108 billion in capital expenditures over that stretch, trying to challenge Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. But it still has no marquee customer for its most advanced chips and is primarily manufacturing chips for its own products.

Wall Street is losing patience. After the U.S. government took a 10% stake in Intel in August 2025, and Nvidia followed with a $5 billion investment, the stock started to shoot up, ultimately rising sixfold over 10 months before peaking in June. Intel was finally finding its niche in the artificial intelligence boom, delivering central processors used for agentic AI, while optimism simultaneously grew over its fab aspirations.

Intel's story took a dramatic turn for the worse on Thursday, when the company said revenue this quarter will be between $1 billion and $2 billion shy of analysts' estimates as the PC market continues to suffer from soaring costs for memory and other components. Intel still counts on its PC segment for roughly half of its revenue, and consumers are increasingly choosing not to upgrade.

The stock plummeted 29% on Friday, its worst day since the 1970s, capping off a 45% plunge in October, the steepest monthly drop on record, according to FactSet. It's down another 8% in Monday trading.

Intel's investments in the future are supposed to be paying off. CEO Lip-Bu Tan said he sees the global market for server processors increasing as much as five times over the next five years to about $200 billion. Intel's data center business recorded 78% growth in the third quarter from a year earlier to $7.3 billion.

However, Intel said it can't make enough chips to fill demand, pointing to industrywide supply constraints for wafers, substrates and memory, many of the same factors hurting PC sales.

That's forcing Tan to make some hard choices. In response to the capacity shortage, Intel said it's shifting some Oregon factory capacity away from making wafers for its advanced 18A chips to make more wafers on an older node, known as Intel 3, used for its data center products.Intel is counting on 18A to attract outside foundry customers, so the shift in resources could complicate that effort. David Yoffie, a Harvard Business School professor who previously spent almost three decades on Intel's board, said the latest moves are hardly welcoming to prospective foundry customers.

"They're concerned that if Intel doesn't actually have excess capacity beyond its own demand, that they're just not a very credible supplier," Yoffie said. If it doesn't find a large-scale foundry customer within a year, "then the stress on Intel's economics are going to start to show even more dramatically," he said.

An Intel spokesperson said in a statement that "customer confidence in Intel Foundry is built on execution."

"We're delivering against our technology roadmap, demonstrating strong performance with 18A and progress on 14A," the spokesperson said, referring to the company's forthcoming chips. "We continue to see strong engagement with customers and remain focused on earning their business through consistent execution."

Intel is best known for its industry-leading central processing units, or CPUs. Graphics processing units from Nvidia and Advanced Micro Devices ruled the early AI boom because they excel at handling large workloads for training models and inference for chatbots, while CPUs like Intel's are crucial for running the virtual machines used by agentic apps like Meta's Muse.

As one of the only companies in the world capable of manufacturing and packaging the advanced chips it needs for its own products, Intel has "more control over their destiny" than most chipmakers, Jordan Klein, an analyst at Mizuho Securities, said in an interview.

"If you don't have enough capacity, you leave revenue and money on the table," Klein said.

Former Intel CEO Pat Gelsinger, who was ousted in late 2024, spent his four-year tenure at the company aggressively pursuing foundry investments to catch up after delays on its 7- and 10-nanometer technologies, advanced process nodes that aim to fit more transistors on each chip.

Tan has taken a more cautious approach, limiting spending until customer demand is locked in. He said on Thursday that "a couple customers" would be announced in the coming two quarters.

Intel's 18A chips, for its own use, are in high-volume production out of Arizona, with a second fab in the works. A first structural truss was just installed at Intel's new Ohio facility, although the project is nearly five years behind schedule.

Another roadblock to getting new customers, Yoffie said, is that chipmakers like Broadcom and Qualcomm may hesitate to manufacture chips at a company that's also a competitor on the product side.

"The right solution is for Intel to spin off the foundry business," Yoffie said, calling it "not only the best solution, but maybe the only solution."

AMD reports third-quarter results after the bell on Tuesday.

Yoffie said the U.S. needs Intel to succeed.

"Ultimately, TSMC can only create maybe 20-25% of their global capacity in the United States and that will still take several years," he said. "If we have no American foundry and we run into any number of geopolitical problems, supply chain problems like we had under Covid, that is going to be bad for the country and bad for the American semiconductor industry."

#2941. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2937. 13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season

13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles aren't even at the halfway point yet.

There are still nine games to go in this miserable 2026 season. And now that the Eagles are sitting at 2-6 with a point differential of -65 it probably feels a little daunting to have to watch this team play nine more times.

Maybe some Eagles fans really will do something else on Sundays — not to mention Thanksgiving and Christmas Eve — but let's be honest: Most of you are still going to watch when the Eagles play.

So other than rooting for a good draft pick, how do you find enjoyment out of a lost season like this? The answer is pretty easy. You get to monitor the development of the young players on the team. And, hopefully, you'll be able to see their progress in the second half of this season.

With that in mind, here are 13 young Eagles who are worth watching the rest of the way:

QB Joe Milton: Maybe the Eagles really do just ride it out with backup quarterback Tanner McKee in place of Jalen Hurts. But there's some definite intrigue with Milton. The Eagles signed him off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16 and it didn't take long for Milton to supplant Cole Payton as the new backup. The 26-year-old Milton (6-5, 236) has played in just five career games but the former sixth-round pick has plenty of arm talent. His highlight reel from his 2023 season at Tennessee is littered with highlight plays. Milton has a big arm and can elude pressure. He just wasn't very consistent. But in this lost season, if McKee continues to struggle, it would be fun to see Milton get a chance.

QB Cole Payton: The Eagles on Sunday night made an interesting decision. They kept Cole Payton active over offensive lineman Jake Majors but Payton didn't play. My first thought was that Payton would have a role on special teams but he didn't. So perhaps there was an offensive role in mind for Payton that the Eagles didn't get to in their 27-10 loss — or maybe he was just an emergency RB option. Payton is a heck of an athlete and while he's developing as a QB behind the scenes, maybe there's a chance to get him on the field in some other roles.

RB Jaydon Blue: In the loss to the Commanders, Blue played just 8 snaps on offense and 7 snaps on special teams, despite Saquon Barkley being banged up and others missing. Blue 22, was a fifth-round pick out of Texas last year but played in just five games with the Cowboys as a rookie. Blue has an intriguing skillset. He ran a 4.38 at the Combine last year and also showed an ability as a receiving threat at Texas. He's a different kind of back and it would be fun to see him get some burn.

WR Makai Lemon: The first-round pick from USC got off to a slow start in his rookie season but has been getting better and better once the Eagles scrapped the portion of the playbook that had him going backwards. Over the last couple of weeks without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks, Lemon has played more and has had his two biggest games of the season. Overall, Lemon has 31 catches on 45 targets for 251 yards and a touchdown. Among rookies, Lemon is sixth in receiving yards behind Carnell Tate, Denzel Boston, KC Concepcion, Germie Bernard and Antonio Williams. But Lemon has been coming on stronger recently.

WR Darius Cooper: The UDFA from Tarleton State is still just in his second NFL season. He made the roster again in 2026 and has 11 catches for 175 yards and 4 touchdowns this year. But he has caught just 45.8% of his targets, which is a very low number. Cooper hasn't been a star but he has made some big-time catches and should continue to get some run the rest of the way.

TE Eli Stowers: The second-round pick from Vanderbilt missed the first six games of the season on Injured Reserve with a quad injury. But Stowers made his NFL debut in Week 7 and then made his first NFL catches in Week 8. A couple of those catches came in garbage time but it was still good to see him finish with 3 for 53. When the Eagles drafted Stowers, we knew he was a developmental tight end. The best way to develop is to get him more playing time this season. Stowers is far from a finished product but he has the type of athleticism that's worth developing.

OL Willie Lampkin: The Eagles have had Landon Dickerson back in the starting lineup at left guard the last couple of weeks, but it'll be interesting to see if Dickerson remains with the team through the trade deadline. If not, maybe we see a bit more of Lampkin. The 5-foot-11 offensive lineman started a couple of games at left guard in between Cam Jurgens and Dickerson. Even if Lampkin can't get back on the field as a starter, the Eagles could find some unique ways to use him. He lined up as a fullback on the Saquon Barkley direct-snap touchdown on Sunday night.

OG Micah Morris: The sixth-round pick from Georgia has played just one snap all season so he probably won't get many chances to play this season. But if more injuries hit, Morris could get some chances. He's a really athletic rookie who is worth developing.

C Drew Kendall: The Eagles drafted Kendall out of Boston College in the fifth round last year and have been high on him for a while. We saw that this year when they got him into the starting lineup from Week 2. Kendall has now started five games at center this season and could be the Eagles' long-term fix at the position. The Eagles traded away his predecessor Cam Jurgens earlier this season, so if Kendall plays well the rest of the way, he could keep that job for a while.

OT Markel Bell: The third-round pick from Miami has had some ups and downs this season. Nick Sirianni seemed impressed by Bell's performance against the Cowboys, but Bell didn't have his best game against the Commanders. Lane Johnson started the first three games of the season before he abruptly retired and Bell has now started the last five games. According to PFF, Bell has given up 17 pressures and 5 sacks this season. He has had some good moments in the run game too. Johnson seemed really impressed by Bell in training camp; we'll see if Bell ends up being his long-term replacement.

DT TY Robinson: Robinson played 104 defensive snaps as a rookie fourth-round pick last year. Through eight games this season, Robinson has played just 52 snaps and is firmly behind Jalen Carter, Jordan Davis, Moro Ojomo and Byron Young on the depth chart. Robinson has played just 9% of the snaps, while Young has been on the field for 27%. Robinson had a solid training camp and preseason so it might make sense to start having him eat into Young's snaps.

LB Jihaad Campbell: This is a tricky one because Campbell began the season as a full-time starter next to Zack Baun and then the Eagles started up a rotation between Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. While it looks like Trotter might simply be the best option right now, Campbell was a first-round pick and has a ton of athletic ability. Campbell hasn't lived up to expectations but he's in Year 2 as a first-round pick. The only way for him to get better is to continue to play.

S Drew Mukuba: Mukuba is in a similar position as Campbell but there's no one like Trotter pushing him. The second-year, second-round pick from Texas has had a mostly disappointing season as a full-time starter in 2026. But he did have a good game against the Commanders with a forced fumble and an interception that was negated by a penalty. Through eight games, Mukuba has played 556 of 558 snaps. We might learn a lot about him over the final nine games of the season.

#2934. Timberwolves 112, Bucks 100: Ball-ing and Bowling to the Rim

After a grueling week that included five games in seven nights and two back-to-backs, the Timberwolves got back to a normal scheduling rhythm as they kicked off the week against the Milwaukee Bucks. Minnesota took down Milwaukee in a preseason matchup as the Bucks have continued to try to find themselves in their post-Giannis era. They've had a lot of difficulty starting the season, including losing their last two games by 20+ points.

An already depleted Timberwolves roster was down another rotation player in Terrence Shannon Jr., due to a hip contusion, as the injury bug has found its way to the Twin Cities. The border battle clash always provides a little extra fuel, even when the teams are in opposing conferences.

LaMelo Ball, looking frustrated from his recent struggles, came out flame-throwing as the Bucks played drop and allowed him free rein to shoot behind physical Rudy Gobert screens. Ball drove the offense, hitting nothing but nylon on the majority of his shots. He drove the offense for the beginning of the game.

After a half-hearted defensive stretch led to some Bucks triples, Jaylen Clark and Joan Beringer came in and slammed the door. Minnesota kept the defensive intensity up but struggled to put the ball in the hoop after Ball went to the bench, but they maintained a 29-18 advantage after the first 12 minutes.

LaMelo Ball continued to drive the offense as he scored half of their first-half points while being aggressive in looking for his shot. Sloppy rebounding allowed Milwaukee to get back into the game as they closed the gap throughout the quarter.

There were a few stretches where Minnesota lost focus, and it allowed the Bucks to crawl their way back. The sloppiness persisted, and Tyler Herro hit back-to-back threes to close the second as the Bucks sliced into the double-digit Timberwolves advantage and cut the lead to one point.

The Bucks killed the Timberwolves on the glass and created second-chance points. They led that category 16 to 5 at the half.

LaMelo Ball scored 24 points while Jaden McDaniels trailed far behind as the next highest scorer with seven points. Dosunmu filled up the stat sheet in the other categories with seven rebounds and five assists.

Jaden McDaniels started the quarter off on the wrong foot as he got two fouls in the first 20 seconds of the third that sent him to the bench. Ayo Dosunmu took the baton from LaMelo Ball to start the quarter as he was bowling his way to the rim and finishing with feather-like touch. Tyler Herro matched that output as he stayed hot from his close to the second quarter, as the Timberwolves offense sputtered on the other end. The Bucks were getting too easy of looks as the game went back and forth during the period. Trey Lyles also left his mark, scoring 10 points in the third quarter.

It was a close start to the 4th quarter before Joan Beringer and Jaylen Clark gave the Timberwolves a shot in the arm. The Bucks were on a cold streak that Minnesota couldn't seem to shake for much of the game before this point. Those two helped to get some separation and grew the lead to double digits.

Milwaukee decided that they couldn't score with Rudy Gobert out there, nor were they able to stop the Timberwolves' offense, so they deployed the hack-a-Rudy strategy. That somewhat worked for them, as they were able to get Gobert out of the game, but Minnesota battened down the hatches and knocked down a few triples to close out the game for a 112-100 victory.

The Timberwolves themselves were reeling after the Anthony Edwards injury as they did not know who was next up in the team's hierarchy. There were multiple quotes that emphasized this sentiment, but it was always clear who would need to step up with their pack leader sidelined: LaMelo Ball.

Ball struggled initially taking the reins of the team that needed him to have his playmaking take a step back so his shooting could take the shine. He struck that balance in this one. He came out of the gates against the Bucks scorching hot, and kept the pedal down for the entire game.

LaMelo had half of the team's points at halftime and found ways to get his shot off while also finding ways to beat his man off the dribble and take it all the way to the basket. He scored 34 points on 12/21 shooting while adding a 6/13 day from beyond the arc. The fouls almost caught up to him in the second half, but his shooting aggressiveness is exactly what this offense needed.

Ayo Dosunmu is a jack of all trades while being a master at diving at the rim and finishing with a soft touch. While LaMelo was focused on putting the ball in the hoop in this one, Ayo took his role as a facilitator, setting up his fellow Wolves with easy looks all around.

He scored 19 points, added 12 rebounds and 8 assists in a stat-sheet-stuffing night. He is the king of scaling his game up and down to fit whatever the team needs, and did that in the third quarter tonight. When LaMelo was struggling to get going, and the Bucks were fighting back, he calmly took the ball and used his patented straight-line driving and love tapping off the glass to stem the Milwaukee tide.

He is a key part of the Ant-less Timberwolves, and more games where he finds himself all over the court will do the Timberwolves well in this stretch.

The Timberwolves travel south for a matchup against the rugged Houston Rockets (6-1). That game can be streamed on DAZN at 7:30 pm CST.

#2933. Trump Account holders to receive SpaceX stock from Gwynne Shotwell in December

Eligible Trump Account holders will begin receiving donated shares of SpaceX stock next month, according to an X post on Friday.

"Robert and I are thrilled that our gift of over 2 million shares of SpaceX stock will be distributed to children's Trump accounts in December!" President and COO Gwynne Shotwell wrote in the post.

The gift from Shotwell and her husband will provide a share apiece to around 2 million Trump Accounts holders ages 11 to 17 who live in ZIP codes with median family incomes of $57,000 or less, or in ZIP codes with too few residents for a median family income to be reported by the U.S. Census Bureau. These families should receive a notice by Monday, Shotwell wrote on X.

"In a few weeks, an additional gift will be finalized for eligible 11 to 17-year-old children in military families serving overseas, children in foster care, and more children whose MFI is $90,000 or less living in counties near our home in the great state of Texas," she also wrote.

Parents or guardians can check eligibility for the donation through Invest America, a nonprofit advocacy group, and track account activity with the Trump Accounts app.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may also receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the U.S. Department of the Treasury.

Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria. In a separate funding announcement in October, Elon Musk wrote in an X post that he plans to give every kid in America a share of SpaceX sometime in 2027, which may also come via Trump Accounts.

The U.S. Department of the Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold donated individual stocks, a change from previous guidelines that only allowed diversified, low-cost index funds.

The move to permit stock donations could pave the way for more large-scale philanthropic gifts, Internal Revenue Service CEO Frank Bisignano told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Oct. 8.

On Oct. 29, Bisignano announced his own donation to Trump Accounts for eligible children ages 2 to 8 living in 11234, Bisignano's hometown ZIP code, according to The Bisignano Family Fund.

#2926. Sixers Bell Ringer: Widespread effort from starters lifts Sixers past Bulls

Bell Ringer Standings:Tyrese Maxey – 3.5Joel Embiid – 1LeBron James – 1Anfernee Simons – 1Jaylen Brown – 0.5

The Philadelphia 76ers had all five starters score in double-digits and three score more than 20 points in a 126-107 win over the Chicago Bulls on Monday night.

The Sixers were almost completely healthy for this one, with the only absences being Dominick Barlow (back) and Ariel Hukporti, who is out for the season. Taking on a struggling Bulls squad that came into Monday 1-5 on the season, they had a golden opportunity to get a comfortable win and maybe even get the starters some rest if they could put it to bed early.

Of course, it can never be too easy. After letting Chicago hang close in the first half, the Sixers finally started to put a little distance between them going into the final frame leading by 11. Philadelphia couldn't seem to fully shake loose until late in the fourth when they extended their lead to 20 with two minutes to play. It would have been nice to gain steadier footing early to get the starters some rest before that point, but I guess we can't be too greedy talking about a now 7-1 team.

Next up, the Sixers will hit the road for three games, the first of which will take them to Detroit to take on the Pistons on Wednesday night.

Until then, let's get to the Bell Ringer. It was a widespread effort, so we have a few options!

VJ Edgecombe: 21 points, 3 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal, 1 block

(Photo by Jesse D. Garrabrant/NBAE via Getty Images)

To anyone who says that VJ Edgecombe can't thrive in this Sixers' lineup, please watch the first quarter of tonight's game. It was the perfect example of a simple but effective synergy between the sophomore and his new-look squad — VJ fully utilizing his speed in transition to get out ahead with LeBron James and Tyrese Maxey getting their heads up to nail impressive passes to him in stride. Edgecombe took full advantage, shooting 5-for-5 from the floor (1-for-1 from long range) for a floor-leading 11 points in the first frame. And if it ain't broke, don't fix it. LeBron hit another cross-court dime to a pushing Edgecombe to help the boy from Bimini to 15 points on 7-for-7 field goal shooting by halftime.

This is why I never worried about Edgecombe's development in this lineup. Sure, there's only one ball… but when you have passers like James (and Maxey, though to a lesser extent) around you, there will always be opportunities for a speedy, athletic player that is constantly in motion off the ball. Tonight was the perfect example of how he can become borderline uncoverable. And if you move (and finish) like Edgecombe did tonight, one can imagine those opportunities will only become more plentiful as the Sixers continue to solidify their chemistry offensively.

Edgecombe finished Monday night with 21 points on 9-for-12 field goal, 2-for-4 three-point shooting. He also had three rebounds, five assists, a steal and a block.

LeBron James: 15 points, 7 rebounds, 9 assists, 1 steal

LeBron James hit not only one but several passes in this game that I watched on repeat. And he made them look easy. I'm talking multiple full-court bounce passes that hit blazing fast players like VJ Edgecombe perfectly in stride and a number of perfectly timed dimes in the half-court offense working off double (or even triple) teams. I'll be honest, I didn't watch LeBron play night in and night out before he came to Philadelphia… it is an absolute treat to watch the plays he makes for his teammates every single game, with the correct passes at the correct time to the correct players to make the most of each moment. By the time an opponent's shot is in the air, James is already positioning himself to grab the rebound and to be able to make a quick, accurate pass ahead.

It sounds like I'm overstating things, but I genuinely don't think it can be overstated what a facilitator he's been to start this season.

In addition to his nine dimes, LeBron found the time to sink some buckets of his own, chipping in 15 on 6-for-13 field goal shooting. He also pulled in seven rebounds and had a steal. His first triple-double as a Sixer still eludes him for now, but something tells me it won't be too long…

Tyrese Maxey: 26 points, 4 rebounds, 11 assists, 2 steals, 2 blocks

(Photo by Jesse D. Garrabrant/NBAE via Getty Images)

Philadelphia rained down MVP chants for Tyrese Maxey when he went to the line at one point tonight… and frankly, they're not crazy. Well, maybe they are, but not for this! Maxey continues to be an absolute baller to start this season, and notably it's coming in different ways and on both ends of the floor. Not only has Maxey's defense been incredibly impressive these first eight games… and not only has he been an absolute scoring powerhouse at times… but he's also taking some clear queues from LeBron James and hitting some monster passes as well.

With such a widespread scoring effort tonight, it didn't have to be all on Maxey's shoulders to chip in 40 points. But even without the overwhelming point total tonight (not that 26 is anything to be ashamed of), it's amazing just how many of the Sixers' points Maxey played a large part in, whether he scored them, assisted on them, or came up with the defensive play that led to them. First, he got his team going early. Then, he was scoring in bunches himself by the fourth.

Maxey finished Monday with a 26-point, 11-assist double-double. He shot 10-for-15 from the floor. He also had four rebounds, two steals and two blocks.

Jaylen Brown: 25 points, 11 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal

It was another night of Jaylen Brown just being Jaylen Brown. I'm sounding like a broken record across these Bell Ringers, but it's incredible how much Brown contributes each game in a somewhat quiet manner. It's not the high-flying dunks of VJ Edgecombe or the cross-court passes of LeBron James. It's not always one big spurt of scoring where Brown puts the Sixers on his back for a few minutes. It's just simple, constant contribution when he's on the floor, on both ends of the floor. You hear his name called by Matt Cord in the arena so often you almost stop hearing it. He just balls. Tonight, it was 25 points on stellar shooting, 9-for-15 field goal and 4-for-7 from beyond the arc, spread out pretty evenly throughout the entirety of the game. This level of performance has seemingly already become the accepted, unceremonious norm from Brown on the Sixers… and that's something worth taking note of.

Brown finished the night with a 25-points, 11-rebounds (5 offensive) double-double with 5 assists and 1 steal. He hit four triples, two of which game back-to-back late in the game which really felt like the dagger in this one (finally).

#2925. Jim Cramer says these six fashion stocks are buys beyond the AI trade

CNBC's Jim Cramer highlighted six fashion and apparel stocks on Monday for investors looking for alternatives to the struggling AI trade.

While technology stocks have come under pressure in recent weeks, consumer stocks have been resilient. The SPDR S&P Retail ETF (XRT) gained 10% this quarter, while the Technology Select Sector SPDR Fund (XLK) fell 11%. Cramer said falling interest rates could provide an additional boost to apparel companies heading into the holiday shopping season.

"Ralph Lauren, Tapestry, Levi's, VF Corp, Kontoor Brands and Reformation are some of the fashion stocks that we think look best right now," the "Mad Money" host said.

Ralph Lauren remains Cramer's favorite apparel stock, thanks to its consistent growth under CEO Patrice Louvet. The company has continued gaining momentum across women's apparel, accessories and outerwear, and has been strengthening its international presence, particularly in Asia. Although the stock pulled back in August and September, it has since recovered, and Cramer is optimistic ahead of Thursday morning's earnings report.

Tapestry, the parent of Coach and Kate Spade, also reports Thursday. Cramer likes Coach's position in the accessible luxury market, particularly its popularity among younger female consumers. However, shares have fallen 20% since the company's August earnings report, when guidance failed to impress investors following a massive multiyear rally. "I hope this Thursday's report can represent the start of a comeback for Tapestry," he said.

Levi Strauss is another potential turnaround story. Shares fell below $20 following disappointing earnings a few weeks ago, but CEO Michelle Gass told Cramer that the company had moved quickly to address inventory shortages in popular styles. The stock has since rebounded more than 20% from its post-earnings low, though Cramer still considers it inexpensive at roughly 14 times earnings, with a 2.8% dividend yield. "I think this is a good option to play the broader strength in denim that we keep hearing about," he said.

VF Corp, which owns The North Face, Vans and Timberland, is showing signs of improvement after years of struggles, particularly at Vans. Cramer pointed to encouraging results from a recent Piper Sandler teen survey and a strong quarterly report as evidence that the sneaker brand's turnaround may be gaining traction. Shares have risen roughly 14% since he recommended the stock about two weeks ago.

Kontoor Brands offers another opportunity tied to denim and corporate restructuring. The company plans to sell its Lee brand for up to $1 billion, allowing it to focus on Wrangler and outdoor apparel maker Helly Hansen. Cramer expects the sale to be followed by substantial share repurchases and said the remaining businesses have room to grow. Shares trade at roughly 14 times earnings and offer a dividend yield near 3%.

Cramer's more speculative pick is Reformation. The women's fashion brand initially struggled after its IPO in late July, but Cramer said it "has great growth, it's profitable, and it has a differentiated shopping experience with a technology kicker." Cramer highlighted the stock in late September; shares surged more than 31% in October.

#2919. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2917. I bonds now pay 5.51% — but there are trade-offs. What to know before buying

Now that Series I bonds pay higher interest, the government-backed, nearly risk-free asset could be more attractive for certain investors. But I bonds have downsides, and there are alternatives to consider.

Newly purchased I bonds currently pay 5.51% annual interest through April 30, up from the 4.26% yield offered on purchases made through October.

Amid rising inflation, the variable part of I bond interest rose to 4.08%, while the fixed part increased to 1.40%, the highest since 2006. Combined, the 5.51% composite rate is the biggest yield since November 2022.

The new I bond rate is "very attractive," according to Ken Tumin, founder of DepositQuest.com, a blog that tracks I bond rates, among other deposit accounts.

The variable part of I bond interest resets every six months, based on the consumer price index. For the next six months after purchase, I bonds look "very competitive" compared to other shorter-term options for cash, such as savings accounts or certificates of deposit, according to Tumin.

Meanwhile, the I bond's 1.4% fixed rate stays the same for as long as you own it, which could appeal to longer-term investors, he said.

But I bonds aren't right for everyone. Here are some key things to know before purchasing.

Investors can buy $10,000 of electronic I bonds per person per calendar year via their TreasuryDirect account. You can open additional TreasuryDirect accounts for other family members or businesses to buy more.

I bond interest is tax-deferred until redemption and is not subject to state or local taxes. You can also avoid federal taxes if you use the money for qualified education expenses.

But experts say there are some downsides. The funds are locked up for 12 months from purchase, and you'll lose your last three months of interest if you redeem within five years.

Advisors typically recommend keeping at least three to six months of living expenses in cash for emergency savings. I bonds could boost those reserves, experts say, as long as you keep enough readily accessible cash elsewhere.

"I always say [I bonds are] a secondary emergency fund," said David Enna, founder of Tipswatch.com, a website that tracks Treasury inflation-protected securities, or TIPS, and I bond rates.

Once you own I bonds for five years, you can redeem the money as needed, penalty-free.

With I bond purchase limits, restrictions and some TreasuryDirect complaints, advisors often recommend other options for clients who want to preserve purchasing power.

"For most of my clients, I bonds are more hassle than they're worth," said certified financial planner Eileen Olson, founder of advisory firm Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina. "The amount is too small to matter."

On a $2 million portfolio, the $10,000 annual purchase limit is only 0.5%, and it earns about $550 in a year before federal taxes, she said. "That won't protect anyone's lifestyle from inflation."

Instead, Olson prefers TIPS, which don't have purchase limits and can be bought alongside other assets, outside TreasuryDirect.

TIPS have five-, 10- and 30-year terms, with yields ranging from about 2.3% to above 3% for the 30-year term, as of Nov. 2.

However, the right option depends on your investing goals and timeline, experts say.

"For short-term cash, Treasury bills and high-yield savings are simpler," Olson said.

Also backed by the U.S. government, Treasury bills, or T-bills, have terms ranging from four weeks up to 52 weeks. As of Nov. 2, some T-bill yields were well over 4%.

By comparison, the top 1% average of high-yield savings accounts were paying above 4%, according to rate comparison site DepositAccounts. Some one-year certificates of deposit were also offering rates over 4%, the same day, according to Bankrate.

Money market funds are another option. Some of the biggest funds were paying around 4%, as of Nov. 2, according to Crane Data.

#2914. Rapid Recap: Timberwolves 112, Bucks 100

After fighting for most of the game, the Milwaukee Bucks stalled late, letting the Minnesota Timberwolves ride a dominant final four minutes to a 112-100 win. The Bucks slump to 1-6 for only the fifth time in franchise history, extending their losing streak to three games. Tyler Herro led the Bucks in scoring, posting 21 points on 8/18 shooting. Jaime Jaquez scored 19 and added eight rebounds, while Myles Turner and AJ Green each added 13 points. LaMelo Ball led all players with a season-high 34 points on 12/21 shooting, while Ayo Dosunmu (19 points, 12 rebounds, eight assists) had a near triple-double.

Taylor Jenkins tweaked his rotation on Monday, dropping Ryan Rollins from the starting lineup in favor of Brayden Burries. Kyle Kuzma fired a bullet to an open Jaime Jaquez in the paint, who easily rolled it in for a layup to start the scoring. However, led by back-to-back long threes from LaMelo Ball, the Wolves rattled off 10 unanswered points to take an eight-point lead inside the first three minutes. Ball connected on his third triple of the game with just four minutes elapsed, putting the Timberwolves up 15-4 and forcing a Bucks timeout. Milwaukee quickly worked their way back out of the break, going on a 9-0 run behind triples from Jaquez, Burries, and Myles Turner; Burries showed the hustle to compete with bigger Wolves defenders on the offensive glass and consistently found teammates on the perimeter. The Wolves responded with a run of their own (12-3), scoring ten points and feasting in transition to lead 29-18 after one.

Impressively, Milwaukee quickly cut the Timberwolves' lead out of the break and even took the lead, with AJ Green connecting on a pair of threes, Rollins getting a driving layup to go, and Burries converting a pull-up. However, Ball caught fire once again, helping Minnesota take the lead right back; the Wolves' star PG scored 11 second-quarter points on 4/7 shooting, and his presence created so many good looks. However, the Bucks did one thing well: they took away Ball's passing game, with his first assist coming with 1:40 remaining in the third quarter. Anyway, Ball's and-one gave Minnesota a 45-41 lead with 2:51 remaining, but Milwaukee kept it tight, with Herro cashing back-to-back threes in the final minute to cut the home side's lead to one, 48-47, at the half.

Although Minnesota stayed in control for much of the third quarter, Milwaukee kept the game within arm's reach. Myles Turner's triple off a Kuzma kick-out gave Milwaukee a 50-48 lead to start, but Minnesota wrenched it back with an Ayo Dosumnu fast-break layup. Bones Hyland's three gave Minnesota a 68-63 lead with just under five minutes left. Milwaukee and Minnesota kept trading the lead as the game wore on. Trey Lyles gave the home side a nice kick off the bench, knocking down a few bombs late in the third to give Minnesota a 76-71 edge. The Wolves stayed ahead going into the final frame, though a tough Kasparas Jakucionis finish through contact cut their lead to just four at 80-76.

Minnesota slowly built its advantage as the fourth quarter wore on, with Milwaukee struggling with turnovers as they tried to bridge the gap; Joan Beringer intercepted a bad Jakucionis pass, another Jakucionis pass went out of bounds, and Turner lost the ball while trying to draw a foul. These mistakes let the Wolves slowly build a double-digit lead, punctuated by Beringer's put-back layup that forced a Jenkins timeout. Eventually, Milwaukee's offense flatlined as Minnesota picked up steam: Kuzma airballed a three and Kel'el Ware missed a dunk, while a Ball dagger pushed the T-Wolves' lead to 15, their largest of the game. Despite trying some interesting tactics to close the game—including hack-a-Rudy—the Bucks just didn't have enough.

The Bucks shot 30.6% from three-point range, highlighted by an ugly first quarter where they went 3/18 and a fourth quarter where they shot 2/9. The Bucks finished second in three-point shooting percentage (38.9%) last year, and they don't look close to matching that this year.

Brayden Burries needs to start from here on out. The rookie got his first start tonight over Ryan Rollins, who has struggled mightily this season (and was bad tonight, shooting 4/11 with three turnovers). And to be clear, Burries was far from perfect, managing just nine points on 4/15 shooting, but his physical defense and rebounding really stood out.

#2912. Brookfield plans for a $27 billion AI data center at UK power station

Brookfield is targeting a massive artificial intelligence data center in the north of England, as Europe scrambles to develop infrastructure to support the rollout of AI amid high energy and land costs.

Energy company Drax Group said Tuesday it has signed an agreement with Brookfield and the asset manager's neocloud platform Radiant to jointly develop an AI data center on the Drax Power Station estate in North Yorkshire. London-listed shares of Drax were up 8.8% in early morning trading.

The project is initially targeting up to 100 megawatts of capacity to be operational from the first quarter of 2028, with an additional 500 MW of capacity to be brought online between 2028 and 2031. It is then looking to secure more than 1 gigawatt of capacity at the site by an unspecified date.

Brookfield estimates total investment, including semiconductors, could exceed £20 billion ($26.8 billion), with an initial £2.5 billion investment in phase 1.

"This is a unique opportunity for the UK's AI ecosystem to build at scale," Radiant CEO Mahdi Yahya said in a statement. "By designing, building and operating from the UK, it will give British researchers, startups and enterprises access to frontier compute at home."

The first phase of Brookfield's project in North Yorkshire will use power from the grid and target commercial customers from early 2028.

The initial phase is subject to planning, regulatory and network approvals from the government and relevant agencies, alongside securing a tenant for the project, Drax said in a statement said. The company added that final investment decisions are yet to be made.

While the U.S. has surged ahead on developing the huge data centers needed to power AI, Europe has been slower to break ground on the facilities. The U.K. in particular has struggled with high energy costs and challenges with long waiting times for businesses to connect to the grid.

In October, it was reported that Microsoft had pulled out of a deal to anchor an Nscale data center just outside London, amid delays connecting to the country's power grid. An Nscale spokesperson told CNBC at the time that it remained "committed" to the project and was "exploring alternative power sources for the site."

Unlike the U.K., the Nordics benefit from abundant power and available land. Countries in the region, like Finland, have seen major commitments to develop AI data centers this year. In September, Google said it would invest at least 13 billion euros ($15.1 billion) into AI infrastructure in the country, marking the tech giant's largest single investment in Europe.

They would travel roughly 5 miles to take on the reigning champs, facing the team whose stranglehold on their shared city has never been stronger and whose dominance in an uninspired rivalry has been a constant reminder of the chasm between the boroughs.

#2904. Blackstone president calls for more equity to fund AI buildout as debt risks mount

The artificial intelligence infrastructure buildout will need more equity financing as lenders become increasingly exposed to the companies driving the buildout, Blackstone President Jon Gray said Tuesday.

Speaking at the Hong Kong Monetary Authority’s Global Financial Leaders’ Investment Summit, Gray said the scale of capital required for AI is creating concentration risks for debt investors, both across the sector and at individual companies.

Unlike shareholders, who can benefit from a company's growth, lenders have limited upside as their returns are largely confined to interest and principal repayments, he said.

Speaking at the same panel, Pimco CEO Emmanuel Roman warned investors that the scale of AI financing could prove to be "too much" for markets to absorb.

Roman said AI-related capital spending could reach around $10 trillion over the next five years, citing General Atlantic CEO Bill Ford's estimate. He added that about $550 billion in AI debt had been issued in various forms so far this year.

Even with a mix of equity and debt financing, the amount required would be “gigantic,” Roman said, comparing it with a global corporate bond market of roughly $20 trillion.

“At some point in time, it’s going to be too much,” he said.

Global data center demand will require about $8.5 trillion in capital spending over the next five years, Goldman Sachs wrote in a report Monday.

Gray said financing the buildout would require a combination of public markets, banks and private capital, with companies increasingly turning to structured transactions to limit the amount of debt they take on. Cash generated by hyperscalers would also fund part of the spending, he said.

“The creativity is needed because the scale of capital is enormous," Gray said.

Despite those financing challenges, Gray remained bullish on AI infrastructure, saying demand for computing power was “running well ahead of supply.”

Spending on AI models across Blackstone’s portfolio companies had risen 20-fold over the past year as businesses saw financial benefits from uses including coding, customer engagement and content creation, he said.

Meeting that demand remains difficult, however, as securing sites, permits, chips, power and capital becomes more challenging, according to Gray.

"He's just got to keep playing. Everybody goes through those ups and downs throughout the season," Brown said. "And obviously, it's the beginning of the season, so he has to find his way. With everybody, when you're in a funk, you got to find one or two things to hold your hat on, and Mikal is one of the best runners in the game, in both directions, offensively and defensively. So he has to hold his hat on that as well as his cutting and trying to do that and other little things: rebounding, driving and touching the paint and spraying it. And his shot will come eventually."

Bridges was a focal point of the offense early and took his first free throws of the season. But his shot was still misfiring. He played the first three minutes of the third quarter and was benched for the remainder of the second half, with Landry Shamet and Jordan Clarkson eating up most of the minutes.

It was a similar story in Friday's victory over the Bulls, when Bridges logged just 13 minutes. He's averaging a little over six points for the season.

Mikal Bridges (25) of the New York Knicks dunks the ball against the Brooklyn Nets at Madison Square Garden in New York, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. Getty Images

Tyler Kolek was back to third-stringer. Until he wasn't.

After missing four games with a sprained ankle, Jose Alvarado was activated Monday and took his previous position as Jalen Brunson's first backup.

But with New York's offense stuck in the mud, Brown went to Kolek to start the fourth quarter, and it helped push the home team to victory.

Before the game, Brown said it's personal policy to not punish a player for getting hurt.

"I try not to take a guy out [of his rotation spot] due to injury and the reality is Jose got hurt," Brown said.

Monday, Alvarado was scoreless in eight minutes with zero assists. Kolek had two points and an assist in five minutes.

#2899. Patriots injury analysis: Dolphins win creates more questions

Despite playing some bad 30 minutes of football to start their Week 8 game in Miami, the New England Patriots were able to beat the Dolphins with a final score of 24-19. Their ability to overcome a halftime deficit is one of the positive takeaways from the game, but it's not all sunshine and roses.

The team of head coach Mike Vrabel suffered more injuries to some rather important players. Here is our weekly run-down after rewatching the game.

TE Hunter Henry: Catching his third pass of the afternoon in the early second quarter, the Patriots' starting tight end seemingly slipped and exited the field. He showed some discomfort on the sidelines, headed into the blue medical tent and later the locker room. Originally labeled questionable to return with a foot injury, he did reemerge shortly before the half to test out his foot but did not reenter the contest. He went through the same routine in the early third period but once again departed. This time for good: he was ruled out midway through the fourth quarter.

QB Drake Maye: New England's starting quarterback got his left non-throwing hand taped up late in the first half, but did not miss any time. He ended the game having played all 60 offensive snaps, and completing 16 of 20 throws for 199 yards and a pair of touchdowns.

ED Gabe Jacas: Arguably the Patriots' most impressive rookie so far this season, Jacas came up limping after the first third down of the second half. He was initially rushing the passer on the play and reversed course to pursue where the ball was going, and only then started favoring his right knee. The second-round draft pick entered the blue medical tent and was announced as questionable to return. He didn't, even though he remained on the sideline for the remainder of the game.

CB Charles Woods: Woods entered the blue medical tent midway through the third period, after getting banged up on a punt return rep. Lining up as a vice player, there was no obvious collision or moment he got hurt, and no injury was announced either. He reentered the game on the next occasion — a kickoff coverage play eight minutes of game time later — and finished without any notable hiccups. He did, however, stumble while on coverage on a long Dolphins touchdown late in the fourth quarter.

ED Dre'Mont Jones: Jones needed some medical attention on the field after taking a rather violent fall on an end-around halfway through the fourth quarter. He eventually walked off under his own power and ended up missing two plays before a return on the next drive.

The Patriots' skill position depth was already thin before the Dolphins game, and losing Hunter Henry is another blow. To their credit, the unit did adapt and overcome his departure, but there is no denying his importance to the operation either: Henry is one of Drake Maye's favorite targets, and one of New England's most reliable players on the offensive side of the ball.

With him out, rookie Eli Raridon and an already-depleted wide receiver group saw an uptick in snaps. Whether that will continue heading into the upcoming Week 9 game against the Green Bay Packers will be seen; Mike Vrabel did not provide any updates on Monday.

"I don't have any expectations yet," he said. "We'll see how he responds here. He wasn't able to finish the game. So, we'll see where he is. I know Hunter will do everything that he can to get back to help us, and we'll go from there."

The other noteworthy injury coming out of Sunday's game is the one suffered by Gabe Jacas. The fact that he did not leave the field and was never officially ruled out can be interpreted as a positive sign.

However, the first practice of the week on Wednesday will give us further information about the second-round draft pick and the other players listed above. It also will be the first opportunity this week for previously-absent players such as wide receiver A.J. Brown and running back TreVeyon Henderson to return to the field; the same is true for fellow wideout Mack Hollins and fullback Reggie Gilliam, who are now both eligible to return from injured reserve.

The Patriots will host the Packers at Gillette Stadium on Sunday, Nov. 8, for a 4:25 p.m. ET kickoff.

#2898. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.
