#2897. Kentucky is drunk on football. Will Stein has made winning intoxicating

Matt Jones' experience hosting Kentucky Sports Radio told him to expect a few calls or texts about Kentucky basketball on his Monday morning show after UK's first exhibition game.

It did, however, take a backseat on KSR's airwaves that day.

Jones was 37 minutes deep into his show before anyone mentioned basketball.

Jones, his cohosts, and the show's callers and texters had football on the brain.

"It's intoxicating," Jones said on the airwaves that day.

College football's biggest weekend yet? Ranking Week 10's stacked slate of must-see games

For two hours, the conversation fixated on Will Stein's surging football team that, at the time, was fresh off a win against Oklahoma.

Blue blood became another pelt on the wall for the Big Blue.

"On that Monday's show, it would've normally been maybe half football and half basketball, but there was like five minutes (of conversation) on basketball," Jones told USA TODAY Sports. "All anybody wanted to talk about was football. I've never seen it like that."

You'd need to be a fan of a certain age to remember a Kentucky season quite like this one.

Kentucky is 7-1 for the first time since 1977. It's ranked No. 7 in the US LBM Coaches Poll entering a rivalry game at No. 16 Tennessee. Kentucky's strength of record ranks No. 2 in the nation behind only undefeated Miami, according to ESPN metrics.

When you consider the resume, you'd be entitled to believe Kentucky would be ranked higher if not for its name and its history.

"How do you go earn respect? Go win. That's it," Stein told USA TODAY Sports. "And, soon, people will respect this program."

Buoyed by an overtime win against LSU, Kentucky would possess a compelling playoff resume if it wins at least three of its final four games.

Kentucky is finding out what Indiana already experienced: A state crazy about basketball can fall in love with a winning football program.

"I've never seen the city like this, in terms of cheering on the football team like they are right now," senior safety Ty Bryant told USA TODAY Sports.

Bryant is something of an authority on this subject. He's a Lexington, Kentucky, native who previously played for Mark Stoops.

"It just makes me want to continue doing what we're doing," Bryant said of the fanbase's energy. "We have to continue to give them something to cheer about."

It's not that Kentucky's never tasted success. Bear Bryant once served an 11-1 season. Fran Curci's Wildcats finished 10-1 in 1977. Stoops twice delivered 10-win seasons.

Each time, the success proved fleeting. A lack of consistency became Kentucky's bugaboo. So, is this sustainable? Ask Stein or his boss that question, and you'll detect nothing but belief.

"It's completely possible that we can have elite-level football on an annual basis here, that we are in the hunt for championships," athletic director J Batt told USA TODAY Sports. "That is the goal. I can tell you, Will Stein wouldn't be here, and I wouldn't be here, if we didn't think that was a real possibility."

Big Blue Nation is buying in. Kentucky's game against Florida in two weeks will mark a fourth consecutive sellout for an SEC home game. Social media engagement is "through the moon," Batt said, and Kentucky has already generated thousands of new season-ticket sales for next season. Those new sales include existing season-ticket holders adding more seats, past season-ticket holders returning to the fold, or fans putting in for season tickets for the first time.

It adds to the story, too, that Kentucky is coached by one of its own, a 37-year-old wunderkind who grew up watching Hal Mumme's Air Raid.

The day Bryant met Stein, one unmistakable trait jumped out about Kentucky's new coach.

"I seen he was a confident guy from a mile away," Bryant said.

You can't lack for moxie when you're coaching a program that ranks 14th out of 16 SEC teams in all-time winning percentage.

History matters less than ever in this era — ask Indiana — but Stein nonetheless knows the history.

Stein's dad played for Kentucky, and he grew up attending games. Stein's childhood memories are painted with scenes of tailgates and time spent around his dad's former teammates at the K House, Kentucky's game-day hub for its past athletes.

Stein attended the Bluegrass Miracle game in 2002, when Nick Saban's LSU Tigers won on a Hail Mary. He rushed the field as a fan in 2007, when Kentucky stunned No. 1 LSU and Les Miles in triple overtime. LSU went on to win the national championship that season.

That next year, after graduating from Trinity High School in Louisville, Stein became a walk-on quarterback at Louisville, where he started five games throughout his career.

Playing for UK would've been a dream, but the opportunity didn't come. Stein says he harbors no ill will about that.

At Louisville, Stein finished his career playing for Charlie Strong, for whom he'd later work at Texas.

"It all brought me back here to Kentucky, where I dreamt of playing, and obviously it's a dream come true to be the head coach here," Stein said.

Stein's Kentucky roots, his childhood fandom, and his K House memories make for more than a good footnote to this story. Batt considers the coach's roots a legitimate advantage.

"In a time when the window of time you're afforded to build credibility is compressed, authenticity of leadership is even more important," Batt said. "Will's connections to the University of Kentucky, connections to the Commonwealth having grown up here, and just who he is, is very authentic."

Stein's armed with another quality particularly useful to this era: This guy knows quarterbacks.

As Stein's career took off on a meteoric rise these past few years, he proved himself as an ace QB developer.

"We don't just plug a person into the system and hope it works," Stein said. "We literally mold our system to the player that we have."

As Texas-San Antonio's offensive coordinator, he leaned into Frank Harris' dual-threat abilities and incorporated a dose of quarterback draws and zone-read plays.

At Oregon, Stein embraced the empty-set formations quarterback Bo Nix favored.

The next year, he served a dose of tempo offense and run-pass option plays suited to Dillon Gabriel. Next came Dante Moore, who was something of a blend of Nix and Gabriel.

At Kentucky, Stein trusts Kenny Minchey's acumen.

"We're able to put more on him at the line of scrimmage to get us into the best plays possible," Stein said.

Minchey's wits, plus Stein's belief in his quarterback, and a dash of moxie resulted in Kentucky beating LSU at home for the first time since that game Stein attended as a teenager in 2007.

Like most new coaches, Stein leaned into the transfer portal before his first season. His haul ranked among the nation's top-10 transfer classes, according to 247Sports.

Among the newcomers: Minchey, who'd been Notre Dame's blue-chip backup.

Throughout Stoops' tenure, Kentucky typically played tough defense, but it ached for better quarterback play. Insert Stein and Minchey, and consider the problem remedied.

Bryant learned two things about Minchey before the season started: He's very smart, and he knows how to improvise.

"The first time I realized, 'We got something special with this one' was in the spring," Bryant said. "I came on a blitz off the edge. He changed his arm angle and flicked it out there for a completed pass. I was like, 'OK, he's legit.'"

Fast-forward several months, and Stein put the LSU game in Minchey's hands. Stein swiftly held up two fingers after Kentucky scored in overtime. Forget the extra point. He wanted a 2-point conversion and a walk-off win.

Kentucky already had beaten South Carolina when Minchey fired a completion for a 2-point walk-off. What did Bryant think when he learned his coach wanted to run it back with another 2-point walk-off against LSU?

"Awww snap!" Bryant said of his reaction. "In the moment, my heart was beating out of my chest."

At the line of scrimmage, Minchey saw LSU's defense aligned with an empty box as the Tigers scrambled to line up against Kentucky's five-wide set. The play wasn't designed to be a quarterback run, but Minchey improvised, kept the ball himself, and ran into the end zone untouched.

Many of Stoops' best wins occurred on the road. So, Kentucky fans welcomed an overdue cathartic moment at Kroger Field.

Just as they did in 2007, fans stormed the field.

"It was just a collective moment of happiness that really this fanbase hasn't had since COVID, in either football or basketball," said Jones, the radio entrepreneur who's built a big following in the Bluegrass State.

"I used to always say: No fanbase has given more and gotten less than UK football. It's nice to see people get to celebrate some success."

Blake Toppmeyer is the USA TODAY Network's senior national college football columnist. Listen to his podcast, "College Football Unfiltered."Email him at BToppmeyer@gannett.com and follow him on X @btoppmeyer.

#2896. Trump is going to get 'trounced' in the midterms — and knows it, Anthony Scaramucci tells CNBC

U.S. President Donald Trump will get "trounced" in Tuesday's midterm elections, former White House communications director and SkyBridge Capital founder Anthony Scaramucci told CNBC.

Speaking to CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Tuesday, Scaramucci said America is in the throes of a cultural and economic struggle, with the two main parties fighting to deliver "two different visions of where the country should go."

"I think the president's going to get trounced, by the way, and I think he knows he's going to get trounced," he said.

Trump's economic approval rating hit an all-time low last month, according to CNBC's All-America Economic Survey, with 65% of respondents disapproving of the president's handling of the economy, while 80% rated the economy as fair or poor.

"You have one vote. You have one customer relations survey that you can do on the American president — that's at the midterms, and people are not happy," Scaramucci added.

"He promised affordability, he promised no more forever wars, he promised transparency on the Epstein files," Scaramucci said on Tuesday. "He didn't deliver on any of that stuff, and so there's a lot of people inside MAGA that are now also breaking from him."

The White House did not immediately respond to a CNBC request to comment. The White House previously told CNBC that Scaramucci's "relevance ended almost a decade ago." Scaramucci was White House communications director for 10 days in 2017, during Trump's first term.

Prediction markets are now pricing in around a three-in-four chance of the Democrats winning control of the Senate in Tuesday's election. Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

On the Iran war, Scaramucci said he was "bearish" on a potential diplomatic breakthrough after the ballot.

"I don't see any alignment between the Iranians, the Israelis, and the Americans for a potential negotiated solution right now," he said. "If the Americans are going to ramp up the military strikes alongside of the Israelis ... I don't really understand how that's going to necessarily help the region either."

The U.S. launched "defensive" strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island overnight, a U.S. official told MS Now. Trump previously said American forces would not attack Iran before the midterms.

"Wars are very bad for business," Scaramucci told CNBC Tuesday. "They're not great for the stock market. So I would like to have a negotiated settlement, but there's nothing on the matrix where you see the concentric circles lining up, where these three groups would say, 'okay, that's a deal that all three of us are willing to accept.'"

Scaramucci also said "disarray" within the Democratic Party had been "the best thing that has happened for Donald Trump."

"And so, if he gets trounced here, there will be bicameral investigations that will be the main show because there's a lot of things that's going on in the administration that the Democrats are going to want to uncover," he said.

"The Republicans, in my mind, I think there'll be a great intellectual civil war. The Republicans are going to have to reconcile where do they want to go. It's no longer the party of Ronald Reagan, certainly not the party of George W. Bush, but is it the party of Donald Trump after he leaves the stage?"

Scaramucci added that it was going to take a long while to get away from the so-called MAGA movement.

"There'll be DNA elements of MAGA in the next presidential candidate for sure, because looming large over that presidential candidate will be Donald Trump and several hundred million dollars of his PAC money, and so they're not going to want to get too far away from him in 2028. So there will be elements of MAGA, but I also think there'll be a civil war in that party," he said.

#2895. Knicks bench comes alive in groggy win over Nets

For their second time in less than a week, the Knicks' bench picked up the slack for an inconsistent New York team Monday night.

Led by Landry Shamet, Jordan Clarkson, and Miles McBride, the reserves combined for 41 of the Knicks' 103 total points against the Nets, replicating a performance that saved New York against the Chicago Bulls on Oct. 28.

After not scoring a single point in Friday's loss to the Philadelphia 76ers, Shamet, who played for Brooklyn during the 2020-21 season, posted a game-best 22 points, tacking on one rebound and one assist in 29 minutes of play. He also went 6-for-8 from the field, 4-for-6 from beyond the arc, and 6-for-6 from the free throw line.

Clarkson added seven points and a block in 11 minutes, and McBride shot 50 percent both from field goal range and beyond the arc. Though he saw just five minutes of play time toward the end of the game, Tyler Kolek scored two, added one rebound and one assist, and finished the game plus-13.

"Our bench was fantastic," head coach Mike Brown said after the game. "I thought Landry came off and gave us a big lift on both ends of the floor. Jordan as well. And then Tyler — I thought Tyler's minutes were tremendous. He did a fantastic job running the team, getting us some easy looks, and then he was solid defensively."

In a back-and-forth game that saw the crosstown rivals trade leads the whole way through the first 36 minutes of play, the deep bench provided the boost the Knicks needed to get over their third-quarter slump that began before the half. In the 12 minutes after the break, New York allowed a 13-0 run from the Nets and got outscored 35-24.

"When we went into a little funk was at the beginning of the second," Brown said. "Us not scoring gave [Brooklyn] a little hope because they were able to shrink that lead and tie it up, even take the lead. When you give a young team confidence, now it's hard to turn the water off ... So I just started searching at that point and went to Tyler, went to Landry, tried JC, and then we found a group that caught a little groove and helped us take the lead back and create separation."

Brown gave special praise to Clarkson, who recorded a season-high 18 points in 17 minutes, as well as two steals, three rebounds, and an assist against the Bulls. He finished that outing with a game high plus-15 plus/minus.

"You have a veteran that's been there, done that, and he's willing to accept a role that most guys wouldn't accept, even guys younger that haven't proved as much as he's proven in this league," Brown said. "... That's the ultimate team player. I will say this about JC — I've been around some good teammates. He's got to be the best. He's the definition of ultimate pro."

#2894. Chiefs News: Kingsley Suamataia’s benching ‘isn’t punishment’

The Kansas City Chiefs shook up the starting lineup on offense at the last second before Sunday's loss to the Denver Broncos. Veteran backup Mike Caliendo filled in for third-year player Kingsley Suamataia, and according to head coach Andy Reid on Monday, the decision was not made because of anything related to "punishment."

Join fellow Chiefs fans to react to every explosive play and game-changing moment in real time.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

Download the SB Nation app on your mobile device! For Apple devices, click here. For Android devices, click here.

'This isn't punishment' — Kansas City Chiefs HC Andy Reid's comments on Kingsley Suamataia's benching only further fuel the mystery

"Yeah, no, listen, Todd (Leabo), just so we have it straight, I didn't say it was punishment ever," Reid said. "So, this was just between (OL) Kingsley (Suamataia) and I, working some things out here. So, this isn't punishment, and I'll leave it at that."

With Reid leaving room for speculation, fans and observers have filled in the blanks themselves. Two theories have been brought up to me.Theory No. 1: Caliendo is outperforming Suamataia somewhere. This idea suggests that Caliendo is doing something better than Suamataia, whether in the classroom, at practice, or elsewhere. Under this view, the move isn't a punishment at all. The coaching staff simply believes Caliendo is the better option right now.

Kansas City Chiefs' late collapse vs. Denver Broncos opens up AFC West race | ESPN

Reid did have another option, especially with Butker's struggles this season when kicking long field goals. According to NFL Next Gen Stats, a punt would have given the Chiefs the highest win probability ahead of going for it (74.3% vs. 72.5%). A field goal attempt, however, ranked last (71.5%). ESPN Analytics metrics also disagreed with Reid's decision to have Butker attempt the 54-yard kick (73.4% win probability) instead of punting (76.8%).

"It's within Harrison's range," Reid said of his decision.

Butker's miss continued his ongoing woes. Since the start of last season, Butker has missed 16 kicks — 11 field goal attempts and five extra point attempts — the most by any kicker in the NFL during that span.

"It was just a really bad miss," Butker said. "I just completely missed the ball and didn't even give it a chance. It didn't feel good off the foot."

The most troubling part of the Chiefs' loss in Denver is this familiarity | The Kansas City Star

Get this: From the 2018-22 seasons, when the Chiefs trailed by a margin of 4-7 points (more than a field goal but within a touchdown) in the final 14 minutes of a game, they scored touchdowns on eight of 14 drives, a rate of 57.1%.

Since the start of the 2023 season, the Chiefs have seen a similar opportunity — trailing by 4-7 points with less than 13 minutes to go — at the onset of 11 drives.

Guess how many of those photo-finish games they've won? The same big ol' fat goose egg.

Former Chiefs QB Set to Start Against Kansas City at Arrowhead in Week 11 | Heavy Sports

According to a report from ESPN insider Josh Weinfuss, former Chiefs quarterback Gardner Minshew is now the new starter for the Cardinals. That means he is set to play against his former team when Arizona comes into Arrowhead on November 22nd.

Minshew signed a one-year, $1.17 million contract with the Chiefs during the 2025 offseason to back up Patrick Mahomes. When Mahomes went down with his season-ending ACL and LCL injury in Week 15, Minshew started in a Week 16 game against the Tennesse Titans.

Chiefs Stock Watch: Kansas City Failed Patrick Mahomes in Week 8 Defeat to Broncos | Sports Illustrated

Being a nose tackle in the pros is a pretty thankless job. Oftentimes, it's your name not being called that means you're fulfilling your role at a high level. Everyone knew that'd be primarily the case when Khyiris Tonga signed with the Chiefs earlier this year, but he's making a name for himself. After a six-tackle performance against Seattle, the 30-year-old picked up right where he left off.

Tonga finished with just two tackles in Week 8, but one was a run stop on halfback J.K. Dobbins that stood out on tape. He also batted a pass in the second half of play and, per Pro Football Focus, was credited with a pressure on quarterback Bo Nix. On an afternoon and evening that saw several members of the Chiefs fall short, Tonga Time was out in full force.

Rams to open Myles Garrett's practice window after missing 7 games with knee injury | The Athletic

Garrett was the prize of the Rams' offseason, when they traded Pro Bowl outside linebacker Jared Verse as well as first-, second- and third-round draft picks to the Cleveland Browns to land the two-time Defensive Player of the Year. Fresh off breaking the NFL's single-season record with 23 sacks, Garrett arrived to the Rams and helped entice Aaron Donald to end his two-year retirement and return to play alongside him.

The two have yet to share the line together in a game, as Donald missed the season opener while ramping up. That time is coming soon. When it does, the Rams will become the first team in NFL history to have two players with 100 career sacks and a 20-sack season on the same defensive line.

Brandon Aiyuk hires Drew Rosenhaus, wants to play again | Pro Football Talk

Aiyuk has hired Drew Rosenhaus and Rosenhaus told Adam Schefter of ESPN that he is "working on getting [Aiyuk] back to work and playing again in the NFL."

The first step in that process would be filing for reinstatement from the 49ers' reserve/left squad list. Aiyuk went on that list amid extended acrimony with the 49ers about how Aiyuk handled his recovery after a serious knee injury in 2024. The 49ers voided the remaining guarantees on Aiyuk's contract in 2025 and Aiyuk has remained away from the team since that decision.

Jack Campbell injury updates: Lions LB remains in hospital, but 'indications are positive' | CBS Sports

"It was good, it was positive," Dan Campbell said. "Got to see his parents, talk to his wife, and everybody's in good spirits. It's about the recovery, and it's a small setback. That's the family. They're very positive. So that's good in that regard."

5 things we learned from the Chiefs' heartbreaking collapse in Denver

#2891. A little-known biotech’s high-stakes cancer bet just paid off and its shares doubled

Danish biotech Genmab has seen its shares double in three months after a string of major cancer drug trial successes, giving fresh momentum to its push to take greater control of the medicines it develops and sells.

Shares in the roughly $35 billion company surged 48% last week, including a 29% jump in a single session after strong results for petosemtamab, its experimental head and neck cancer treatment. The announcement marked Genmab's third successful Phase 3 trial in October, following positive results for treatments targeting ovarian cancer and lymphoma.

After nearly three decades spent building Genmab into one of Europe's prominent biotechnology companies, CEO Jan van de Winkel told CNBC on Friday the breakthroughs marked "a completely new era."

Historically, Genmab made money from royalties from drugs it helped develop, including Johnson & Johnson's multiple myeloma treatment Darzalex and Novartis' Kesimpta. Van de Winkel said Johnson & Johnson is expected to generate $16.5 billion to $16.9 billion in Darzalex sales this year, with Genmab receiving royalties of up to 20%.

Genmab is now using that financial firepower to build a business that keeps a much larger share of the revenue from its own treatments.

The biggest market reaction followed Genmab's Oct. 28 announcement that petosemtamab, combined with Merck's Keytruda, reduced the risk of death by 50% compared with Keytruda alone in a Phase 3 trial of patients with recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer.

The combination also significantly improved response rates and showed no new safety signals, according to Genmab.

Van de Winkel told CNBC the combination "could potentially set a completely new standard" of care although he declined to say whether the survival data might hold up as the data mature, citing still unpublished full data.

Days earlier, Genmab reported that its medicine Rina-S reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 71% and the risk of death by 57% versus chemotherapy in a Phase 3 ovarian cancer trial.

Barclays analysts said the results exceeded investor expectations and suggested Rina-S could offer an alternative for patients ineligible for AbbVie's competing treatment Elahere, or whose cancer had progressed after receiving it. They cautioned, however, that cross-trial comparisons are limited.

A third late-stage October win came from Epkinly, co-developed with AbbVie, which reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 51% when combined with standard chemotherapy in previously untreated diffuse large B-cell lymphoma.

Analysts see significant commercial potential for petosemtamab, including beyond its initial target market.

Jefferies analysts said the drug could generate $3.5 billion in peak annual global sales treating recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer, and estimated an expansion into locally advanced disease could add another $3.5 billion, an opportunity they believe investors have yet to fully appreciate.

Declining to give specific numbers, van de Winkel said he expects petosemtamab to become a "multi-billion dollar drug."

Genmab is due to report third-quarter earnings on Nov. 5.

Genmab has built its business around antibody-based medicines, including so-called bispecific antibodies such as petosemtamab, which can bind to two different targets on cancer cells simultaneously.

Petosemtamab targets the proteins EGFR and LGR5, and is designed to both disrupt signals that help cancer cells grow and to encourage the immune system to attack the tumor.

Van de Winkel said Genmab's expertise in antibodies and their interaction with the immune system has also helped it spot promising medicines developed by others. It acquired petosemtamab through an $8 billion purchase of Merus in 2025 and Rina-S through a $1.8 billion acquisition of ProfoundBio in 2024.

Those deals are also central to Genmab's changing business model.

It fully owns petosemtamab and Rina-S, while splitting the commercial economics of Epkinly equally with AbbVie, much larger shares than with Johnson & Johnson and Novartis.

"From now on I'm going to increasingly hold on to ownership of these new medicines," Van de Winkel said.

Genmab now has 15 Phase 3 trials across its three lead compounds.

"The model is beginning to be validated and click together," he said. "I think the market gets it at this point finally."

The Merus deal underscored the scale of the bet. Genmab borrowed $5.5 billion to help fund the purchase, relying on its royalty income as a financial backstop if petosemtamab failed.

Owning more of its medicines also means taking on more of the work of selling them. Van de Winkel said the company already has much of the necessary infrastructure in place, having built up commercial capabilities around existing treatments including Epkinly and Tivdak, co-developed with Pfizer.

The company is expanding its sales presence in Europe and seeking to reach more cancer patients through community healthcare, beyond specialist hospitals, in the U.S.

Van de Winkel said he wants Genmab's majority-owned medicines to reach around 500,000 patients by the 2030s, up from fewer than 30,000 today.

He also acknowledged potential interest from larger drugmakers, saying Genmab was likely "high up on the radar screen," but stressed that his ambition remained to build an independent European biotech.

Greater ownership, however, also means bearing more of the cost and risk of clinical trials, regulatory approvals and commercialization.

Van de Winkel acknowledged that geopolitical shifts and drug-pricing pressures could complicate expansion plans and change the order in which Genmab launches medicines in different markets.

#2886. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

Is it too soon to say it? Is the NFC East the Giants' division to lose?

Derrick Henry, now 32, is far closer to the end of his career than the beginning. His contract pays out $12 million in 2027, with none of it guaranteed. Now could be the time for the Ravens to turn the remainder of his contract into a draft pick or two.

Through eight games, Henry leads the league with 158 carries. He has 632 yards (4.0 per carry) and eight touchdowns. Given the quarterback injuries in Baltimore, the Ravens surely will get a call or two about Henry. The Giants, given their interest in Taylor, could be one of them.

New York Giants running back Cam Skattebo was the only player among eight fined for illegal use of the helmet in Week 7 who also drew an on-field penalty.

During the Giants' 27-21 overtime win over the Houston Texans, Skattebo lowered his helmet while running through safety M.J. Stewart in the first quarter. The contact produced a 15-yard personal foul that wiped out a seven-yard gain with New York leading 7-3. The NFL later fined him $7,073, his second such fine of the season after an identical amount for a similar run against the Tennessee Titans in Week 3.

#2881. Stats Rundown: 3 numbers to know from the Mavericks’ grueling 115-100 loss to the Sacramento Kings

The Dallas Mavericks dropped an ugly, grueling game to the Sacramento Kings by a score of 115-110. It was the kind of performance that makes you wonder why any of us spend any time watching sports at all. Dallas, fresh off their most emotional win of the season, laid a massive egg on their home floor and made the winless Kings look like the Harlem Globetrotters.

When such a horrific display of hoops takes place, everyone who steps on the floor is culpable. No one gets out unscathed. But Cooper Flagg, the Mavericks' second-year wunderkind, had the worst game of his career, setting the tone for his supporting cast to follow. Flagg shot just 3-19 from the field and turned the ball over six times. It was an awful showing, the likes of which we won't often see this season.

Unfortunately, the Mavericks compiled a lot of numbers throughout the box score tonight. None of them were good. Nevertheless, it is the duty of the courageous Mavs Moneyball staff and this beleaguered writer to let you know about some of those numbers and why they matter. Without further ado, here are the stats that tell this story of this Kafkaesque nightmare.

Dallas shot 7-of-33 from behind the line while Sacramento went 19—of-43. In the modern NBA, you're not going to win any games when the other team outscores you by 36 from three. With Kyrie Irving resting, the Mavericks were without their best 3-point shooter and ball handler. As a result, space to operate and good looks from three were hard to come by.

As the game went on, the Mavericks got lazier and lazier on defense, too, letting the Kings swing the ball around and get up great looks from distance. The Dallas roster is already short on good shooting, and tonight was a sum-of-all-fears performance.

The Mavericks came into tonight's game fifth in the NBA in fast-break points per game at 18.7. Dusty May is known for pushing the pace in transition as often as possible, but that didn't happen tonight.

Dallas was stuck in halfcourt hell, and things were a slog all night. This team is going to struggle when they can't generate easy baskets.

Let's end the stats rundown on a positive note. There were strong showings from both Daniel Gafford (20 points) and Morez Johnson Jr. (13) tonight, providing unlikely offense for a floundering squad. Gafford kept the Mavericks afloat in the first half and scored 11 points in the second quarter when nothing else was working.

Johnson had a really nice game on both ends of the floor; the Mavericks rookie posted a career-high in scoring while lifting the team defensively. It was a much-needed performance from the rookie big, who has struggled with playing through shin splints. Both Gafford and Johnson have done an admirable job holding down the center spot while Dereck Lively II and Santi Aldama work their way back from surgeries.

#2880. Texas State men's basketball prepared as it faces all-time difficult season in Pac-12

SAN MARCOS - It's no hyperbole to say the upcoming Texas State men's basketball season is the toughest in its history. If anything, it doesn't completely capture the jump the Bobcats - a former Division II program that recently moved from the Sun Belt - are making to the Pac-12.

KenPom ranks four Pac-12 teams in the top-75 of its preseason rankings. Seven are within the top 100, the most among the Group of Six. Meanwhile, the highest-rated Sun Belt team is James Madison, ranked 151st.

The Pac-12 projects to be the best mid-major conference in the country. Yet the Sun Belt hadn't had multiple teams make the NCAA Tournament in over a decade. Competing for a conference championship is a daunting task for head coach Terrence Johnson, who said he is embracing the challenge head-on during media day in late October.

"It's going to be a test, but that's what you want," Johnson said.

Texas State surged late in the 2025-26 season, winning eight of its last 10 games and falling in the first round of the Sun Belt Tournament. The team's core - forward DJ Hall, guard Kaden Gumbs, guard Mark Drone and forward Robert Fields - are back, giving Johnson's roster stability.

But the Bobcats can't thrive on doing the same thing as last year. Gonzaga, San Diego State, Utah State and Colorado State have been NCAA Tournament regulars since the start of the 2020s; Texas State hasn't earned a berth since 1997. It also had the lowest total budget among Pac-12 teams in 2025. It needed talent to keep up.

Texas State Head Coach Terrence Johnson talks to his team as the Bobcats create to play the Texas A&M San Antonio Jaguars at Strahan Arena in San Marcos, Nov. 5, 2025. (Sara Diggins/Austin American-Statesman)

Enter Omaha forward Paul Djobet, Southern Illinois guard Quel'Ron House, UNC Greensboro guard KJ Younger, Boston College guard Caleb Steger and Cal State Bakersfield forward Ron Jessamy. Younger and House both averaged more than 15 points at their previous stops, while the 6-foot-7, 215-pound Djobet adds positional size and versatility to the wing.

"Those three guys in particular, from a number standpoint, I think have a chance to impact winning at a high clip," Johnson said.

The additions might be just what Texas State needs; KenPom ranked them 100th, ahead of Washington State and Fresno State. The Bobcats' freshman recruits include guard Randy Livingston Jr., forward Kole Grandison, forward David Abisogun and forward King Sanders, but with such an experienced roster, it'd take a true standout to crack the rotation.

Speaking of freshmen, Hall enters his sophomore year after an all-time season in which he won Sun Belt freshman of the year award, averaging 15.7 points, 7.7 rebounds and 2.3 assists. His unique style of play might be challenged in the Pac-12. Teams have had a full year to pick apart his game, and Hall will likely face tougher, more skilled competition.

There's room for improvement. Hall could improve his range after going 35.3% from beyond the arc on 17 attempts. He struggled to pick up faster forwards on defense at times last year, and a tighter handle could help him drive and create his shot more easily.

"DJ Hall is going to go up to the very top of the scouting board now," Johnson said.

Beyond the challenges ahead is an opportunity for recognition. According to the Pac-12, 94% of its conference games will be on national TV. Texas State is an underdog, no doubt. But Johnson isn't focusing on how good his opponents might be or the schedule's difficulty. All the coach is doing is molding the Bobcats into the best version of themselves.

"Whether or not we're zigging or they're zagging, I think the best thing is that we're being who we are," Johnson said.

#2879. New national home listing database is taking on Compass in the battle for control of real estate information

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Some heavy hitters in residential real estate just joined the fight over who can see residential property listings. Their goal, they say, is to counter private listings, which are becoming increasingly popular in certain markets.

AGNT (which owns eXp Realty and NextHome), HomeServices of America, Keller Williams and Real REMAX Group, four of the nation's largest brokerages, announced that they are creating a largely open and shared database (API) of listings for brokers — and inviting all other brokerages to join it. Those who do must submit a new listing within one business day. There is, however, an option within the system for certain private listings.

Open Real Estate is designed, according to its founders, to counter the increasingly fragmented residential listing market, and specifically private, off-market listings, which are favored by Compass, a brokerage behemoth. Compass is embroiled in more than half a dozen lawsuits with state and local Multiple Listing Services (MLS) involving listing rules and antitrust claims.

"We want to ensure that for the American consumer, there's a complete data set that is capital T truth, that's reliable as to what is happening in the marketplace," said Leo Pareja, CEO of AGNT. "So whether it's MLSs fragmenting, pocket listings, walled gardens, whatever, the goal is to ensure that there's a complete data set for the American consumer, so buyers and sellers have the most amount of accurate information."

Those who want to join the platform can do so with no initial charge or fees. Pareja said the four founders are going to make the investment into the infrastructure and the technology. Brown Harris Stevens, Lamacchia Realty, Samson Properties and Windermere are listed as initial participants.

"It's clearly a clap back to Compass, firstly," said Jonathan Miller, a longtime housing market analyst and director of markets at StreetMatrix, a housing data and analytics company. "And it's absolutely better because Compass is literally hiding listings from competitors by design."

Just after the announcement, Compass CEO Robert Reffkin posted a congratulatory message on X, saying that Compass is "excited that there will be a national real estate listing service that will return the power back to the creator of the listing — the homeowner and their trusted agent. This is good news for all agents and their clients, and we look forward to helping build the next chapter of listing services."

When asked, Reffkin declined to comment further.

Pareja's response to that: "We saw that, and we're grateful for his appreciation, and he's very much invited. We'd love to have him participate."

While Reffkin's response may seem odd, given that Compass supports private listings, there is one part of the Open Real Estate platform that does allow for exclusives. It's called "participant view." A property that goes into that view is put into a part of the database, "where only agents that are associated with participating brokerages can see it, and it cannot be publicly marketed by anyone, including the listing agent and the listing brokerage," according to Open Real Estate's website.

Reffkin's response came at the same time another MLS, New York's OneKey MLS, joined the California Regional Multiple Listing Service's federal lawsuit against Compass. The organizations are asking the court to firmly establish that rules protecting the shared, public marketing of listings are lawful and pro-competitive, according to a release. It followed OneKey's rejection of Compass's demands to dismantle local cooperation policies.

"This case raises questions that extend far beyond any one company or market," said Richard Haggerty, CEO of OneKey MLS in the release. "It goes to the core of how homes for sale are shared, how everyday buyers access that information, and how we protect a fair, equal housing market for everyone. We believe these issues demand clarity from the court, and joining CRMLS gives OneKey a direct, active voice in securing it."

Separately, last month six regional MLSs representing more than 120,000 real estate professionals nationwide filed a joint federal lawsuit against Compass seeking to uphold rules that promote access to listings through publicly shared databases.

Pareja said the new database is not meant to replace MLSs and that it should not be seen as a national MLS.

"We are going to work with MLSs. We're going to work alongside them. We're not usurping. We're not replacing," he said.

#2878. Warriors' Draymond Green ejected after shove match with Dillon Brooks

SAN FRANCISCO ― There's no love lost between Golden State Warriors forward Draymond Green and Phoenix Suns guard Dillon Brooks.

The two have a history of trash-talking and getting into shoving matches with one another. They kept that tension going during the Warriors-Suns matchup on Monday, Nov. 2.

Green committed a foul against Suns center Khaman Maluach, wrapping him up in an intentional foul before throwing him to the ground. Green was initially called for a foul and then an ensuing technical foul for shoving Maluach to the ground.

Things got spicy and Green got a visit from his old friend, or foe, Brooks. Brooks took exception to Green's push on his teammate and ran to the pile, pushing Green.

Green retaliated as teammates, coaches and game officials had to separate the two players. Both Green and Brooks received double-technical fouls for the dust up.

It was Green's second technical, which resulted in an automatic disqualification. Green left the game at the 10-minute mark of the third quarter. He finished the game with four points, four rebounds, two assists and a steal in 17 minutes.

The Warriors went on to win the game, 116-113.

The Warriors were receptive to Green's ejection. It lit a fire under the team, teammate Stephen Curry said.

"We had to respond so I'm glad we did," Curry said. "It was a combination of that moment. We're going to have out brother's back when he defends us."

Head coach Steve Kerr said he doesn't want actual fights, but rather the team's competitive nature.

"I just want to see the competitive spirit, and I thought we saw that time," Kerr said. "They really punched us in the mouth. You know, that first quarter, they were ready. ... I was thrilled with our competitive spirit. And I know there were a couple dust-ups, but that's just, you know, the greatest NBA basketball players on Earth competing. This is what it is."

Warriors guard Brandin Podziemski said he loved Green's intensity.

"We didn't have the edge to us to start the game," Podziemski said. "Found it a little bit in the second quarter but then they came out in the third quarter pretty well and that kind of changed the game for us. Everybody's locked and came together for Dray. So I think it was needed."

#2876. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2875. Supermarket fridges hit with cyberattacks across Europe — here's what's happening

European supermarkets have become the center of another cyberattack, with hackers remotely heating fridges and spoiling food.

The attack caused temperatures in fridges and freezers to rise sharply, with at least one store reporting temperatures of up to 50°C (122°F) and other retailers discarding food. It's sparked concerns across the retail industry.

Dutch supermarket group Ahold Delhaize reportedly had dozens of Delhaize stores affected in Belgium and Luxembourg.

The Dutch supermarket giant told CNBC that there was an incident with refrigeration systems in dozens of its stores on Monday, which has resulted in food having to be discarded. It said the situation has since returned to normal with stores fully operational.

Meanwhile, Colruyt Group said five of its Spar stores in Belgium were targeted, according to Reuters. French supermarket Carrefour told the BBC that 15 of its Belgian-based stores were attacked too. France's Intermarché also said 15 stores out of 153 in total were impacted in Belgium.

"In most cases, the security measures in place made it possible to quickly contain the attack," an Intermarché spokesperson told CNBC. "However, a few stores did suffer some stock losses, although nothing comparable to the attack in mid-October.

"At this stage, the information available to us indicates that the bulk of the attack affected other players in the sector, notably Carrefour and Delhaize," they added.

IKEA told CNBC that a cyber incident affected refrigerators and freezers at its Mons store in Belgium between Sunday and Monday.

Although the store is still open to customers, the company is inspecting the affected equipment and products as well as checking installations across its other stores in Belgium.

"The incident has a limited impact on the offering in our restaurant and Swedish Food Market," an IKEA spokesperson said over email. "We are continuing our investigation to determine exactly what happened."

ICA, Sweden's biggest grocery retailer, also confirmed that it had been informed of an incident at a "limited number of stores," which were on networks outside ICA's central systems. "The incident has affected the stores' refrigeration and freezer systems. ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the incident," the company said over email.

Elsewhere, Finnish Kesko supermarket, Irish grocery chain Supervalu and Estonian grocer One Selver also reported disruptions to their refrigeration systems.

Carrefour did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Hundreds of French supermarkets were targeted in October in a cyberattack that caused fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil.

Intermarché owner Les Mousquetaires told CNBC at the time that it was expecting damages of several million euros.

"Food security requires more attention at all levels because the weaponization of food has become a reality," European Commissioner for Agriculture and Food Christophe Hansen said on X in October.

Commissaries at six U.S. military bases also suffered freezer malfunctions in late August.

The Pentagon is now treating those as a suspected hack and coordinating with French authorities, four people familiar with the matter told Reuters in October.

In one case, the refrigerator's temperature was raised overnight, leaving masses of food melted and wilted by morning.

Sophos Counter Threats Unit's Senior Director of Threat Intelligence Rafe Pilling told CNBC that while there's no evidence AI has played a role in these attacks, elsewhere criminals have been using AI to speed up the scale and efficiency of attacks.

The attacks have yet to be claimed by any group or bad actor, and investigations are still underway, but it's raised concerns about the vulnerability of critical infrastructure.

Fredrik Blix, strategic cybersecurity advisor for Visente AB, said that the hack itself isn't sophisticated, as the control units for the refrigerators "sit directly on the public internet," with built-in support logins where the password can be obtained on the same day, according to information from researchers published in August.

"The vendor patched it in August," Blix said in a LinkedIn post. "Thousands of units never got the update, and the oldest models can't be updated at all."

He added that France was hit by the same attack three weeks ago, with France's CERT-FR telling Europe's operators to pull the devices off the internet. "The question I put to the suppliers and service companies who install and remotely manage this kit: what exactly did you do with those three weeks?"

Blix noted that the silence behind the attack is the most "interesting clue" as it rules out ransomware crews, leaving two options. Someone either carried out the attack simply because they can, or because someone wants to create "unease in Ukraine-supporting countries without leaving fingerprints," Blix said.

#2873. Russia confirms new Ukraine peace talks as Melania Trump prepares to host ceasefire meeting

Russia on Tuesday confirmed that peace talks with Ukraine and the U.S. will go ahead next week in Abu Dhabi, while also signaling that a meeting between Presidents Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to end the conflict was on the table.

Putin aide Vladimir ​Medinsky will serve ⁠as Moscow's chief negotiator ​at the talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, Kremlin spokesman ​Dmitry Peskov told reporters on a conference call, according to Reuters.

Separately, First Lady Melania Trump will bring Russian and Ukrainian representatives to the White House to discuss the local ceasefire in Kherson, the White House said Tuesday.

Monday saw the end of a six-day ceasefire in the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region to allow for evacuation of residents, in a pause of hostilities that the White House said was brokered by the first lady.

Ukraine on Monday accused Russia of using the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

The first lady's summit on Thursday will be "an extraordinary moment in American history," the White House said, but did not name the representatives who will attend. Melania Trump will deliver remarks on the Oleshky ceasefire at 11 a.m. ET Thursday, it added.

Peskov also said Tuesday the Kremlin hopes to finalize plans for a trilateral meeting between the leaders of Russia, the U.S., and China in the coming days. The comments follow earlier reports that these countries were planning to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

Zelenskyy has said that any negotiations over Ukraine's future must include Kyiv.

"It can be acceptable only when we are at the table of negotiations," he said on Oct. 20, adding that he had discussed the proposal of a three-way meeting between the U.S., China, and Russia to negotiate an end to the nearly five-year full-scale war. "Nothing about Ukraine without Ukraine," he said.

Tensions between the U.S. and Ukraine have mounted over recent weeks amid continued strikes between Moscow and Kyiv, including on civilian and energy targets.

A series of moves by the Trump administration in October raised questions about Washington's reliability as Kyiv's most important military partner.

Tuesday's announcement follows U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff saying late Monday that Washington will mediate Ukraine-Russia peace talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, focusing on security arrangements, de-escalation and humanitarian issues.

Witkoff also said that U.S. negotiators will meet European and NATO officials in Brussels this week to discuss post-war security guarantees and Ukraine's reconstruction.

The Warriors' emotionally charged defensive leader was charged with two technicals on one play early in the second half Monday night against the Suns, sending him to the locker room.

Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors dribbles against the Phoenix Suns in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors draws an offensive foul on Oso Ighodaro of the Phoenix Suns in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. Getty Images

Golden State trailed Phoenix 68-57 at the time he was tossed from the game.

#2871. Jack Campbell is a Lions team captain. Here's why his teammates love him

The sports world was shaken when Jack Campbell suffered a serious neck injury during an NFL game this past weekend.

The All-Pro linebacker got hurt in the fourth quarter of the Detroit Lions' matchup on Sunday, Nov. 1 against the Minnesota Vikings after tackling tight end T.J. Hockenson. Campbell, 26, remained on the ground after the play and medical staff came and tended to him. Campbell was put on a backboard and carted off the field.

Campbell's Detroit teammates were emotional as he was receiving care. Aidan Hutchinson was shown with tears in his eyes.

The Lions won 19-15, handing the Vikings their first loss of the season.

On Monday, Nov. 2, the day after the game, Campbell was still in the hospital, but the team said, "Preliminary indications are positive."

Campbell is a team captain for the Lions and is in his fourth season after Detroit drafted him No. 18 overall in 2023 out of Iowa. Besides being named to the All-Pro team and earning his first Pro Bowl nod last year with 176 tackles and five sacks, he has gained a reputation as an authentic and kind person.

Here's what Campbell's teammates had to say about him:

Although there is no blood relation between Dan Campbell and Jack, the head coach shared some words that show he thinks of the linebacker as family.

"I don't even know if I can put it into words," Dan Campbell said during a press conference the day after the captain's injury, before adding, "He's trustworthy, he's dependable. He'd give you the shirt off his back. He's just all heart, man, all effort. He's just an unbelievable person. Down to earth, very genuine. There's not a man on this team that doesn't respect Jack Campbell and care about him."

Campbell's fellow defensive star Aidan Hutchinson had high praise for the team captain after the game.

"He's everything, man," he said. "He's captain, leader, teammate, husband, father, everything. So yeah, we're all praying for him."

The defensive end also posted a gallery of photos of himself with Campbell on Instagram that several teammates reposted to their Instagram stories.

The quarterback is the de facto leader of any football team, but Jared Goff applauded Campbell for his own role.

"He's a husband and a father and it's scary," he said in a postgame press conference. "... If I know anyone that's tough enough to get through something like that, it's gonna be him."

The fellow Lions linebacker expressed his support for his teammate, who he called a "brother" to the media after the game. He then spoke to his character on his Instagram stories.

"A Real soldier," he posted on social media with a saluting emoji as the caption for an action photo of Campbell.

Jahmyr Gibbs showed his support for his teammate in a few words on his Instagram stories. He reposted Hutchinson's photo gallery expressing his love for Campbell and then posted a photo of himself with Campbell throwing the first pitch at a Detroit Tigers game. He put the caption, "46" with a blue heart.

#2870. 3 key stats from Indiana men’s basketball’s 91-57 win over Eastern Illinois

Indiana men's basketball's 2026-27 season began with a strong 91-57 win over Eastern Illinois on Monday night at Assembly Hall.

The level of competition wasn't all that great, with the Panthers ranking far below all of Indiana's three preseason exhibition opponents in KenPom's efficiency metrics, but there were reasons to feel good about this group heading forward regardless.

Here's three stats that jumped off the box score:

Harris, who signed with Indiana this past offseason after transferring in from Duke, was added largely due to the 3-point shooting ability he showed as a high school recruiting prospect. The Hoosiers made nine threes and none belonged to him.

He went 0-3 from deep on the night. That kind of shooting, especially for a guy whose most impactful trait is floor spacing, can lead to sagging effort. That wasn't the case here.

Harris still finished with seven points, with five coming at the free-throw line. On top of that, he battled for four rebounds. It's promising to see him find ways to affect winning basketball when his shot isn't falling and could be something to watch as he develops throughout the season.

The Hoosiers really got after it on the defensive end, forcing 22 turnovers out of the Panthers while letting up just 13 assists.

Better than that, Indiana turned those 22 turnovers into 29 points, making Eastern Illinois pay for its mistakes. Now, will Indiana be doing stuff like this at this rate in the Big Ten? Probably not the latter, but establishing a strong defensive presence early like this is something you'll take and run with.

In the several errors Indiana forced out of Eastern Illinois, none loom larger than the fact that the Panthers shot just seven free throws on the game.

It's not like they were just going for it from deep either, just 14 of their 50 shots came from 3-point range. Indiana was mostly able to stay out of foul trouble with Samet Yigitoglu, Aiden Sherrell and Sam Alexis each picking up just a single foul apiece.

The sample size is way too small to draw any overarching conclusions, but it's worth watching how Indiana defends the interior in the weeks to come.

#2869. Apple stock sinks as global memory crisis hurts profits, giving Ternus his first big test

John Ternus' first earnings report as Apple CEO was going fine until it was time to issue a forecast for the company's gross margin, one of the key metrics followed by investors.

Apple reported better-than-expected earnings and revenue on Monday, including in the company's core iPhone unit, and even projected sales in the current period that topped estimates. But just before Ternus opened the earnings call up to analysts' questions, CFO Kevan Parekh came forward with the first real piece of bad news.

Parekh said that for the December quarter, which goes through the holiday season, Apple's gross margin will be between 45% and 46%, trailing the 46.3% average analyst estimate, according to LSEG.

"This includes primarily the impact of higher memory costs, as well as some costs related to the ramp of Siri AI," Parekh said.

Apple shares slid 4% in premarket trading on Monday.

It's part of an ongoing trend for Apple, which is absorbing some of the soaring costs from the global memory shortage. A margin drop in the fiscal first quarter would be a third straight decline for Apple, following a gross margin of 50.1% in the June period and 47.9% in the September quarter, boosted by 1 percentage point of tariff refunds.

At the midpoint of the forecasted range, it would be Apple's lowest gross margin since the fiscal fourth quarter of 2023.

"What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented," Ternus said on the call, adding that the company is paying more than it did earlier this year. "Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase."

Ternus, who took the reins from Tim Cook on Sept. 1, is grappling with a problem that his predecessor spent his final months addressing. Cook told The Wall Street Journal in June that the memory situation was a "hundred-year flood." Apple followed by raising prices across the board on Macs, iPads, and accessories.

In September, Apple increased the prices of its iPhones by $100, which Ternus said the company did "reluctantly."

Computer and smartphone makers everywhere are suffering. On Friday, Intel had its worst day on the market since the 1970s due to a weak forecast tied to the softening PC market. It's all because of insatiable artificial intelligence demand.

AI server giants like Nvidia and hyperscalers are buying up all the RAM they can find, and they're willing to pay a premium for it, leaving little supply for companies that make smartphones and tablets.

Despite its size and global clout, Apple said it expects supply constraints on iPhones and Macs in the holiday quarter, primarily driven by memory.

On the call, Ternus had some top lieutenants field component-related questions. Sabih Khan, chief operating officer, became the first Apple COO to appear on an earnings call since Cook in 2010, when Steve Jobs was CEO.

"As we have said before, we're seeing less flexibility in the supply chain than normal," Khan told analysts on the call.

In response, Apple has drastically increased its purchase obligations to secure supply, Khan said. Those obligations rose by $33 billion from the June quarter to the September period, Khan said, though he declined to answer questions about whether Apple had signed "long-term agreements" with memory makers.

"We're always working hard to improve supply," Khan said.

Even with the recent margin slide, Apple's gross margins are still well above where they sat earlier in the Cook era, when the number would predictably come in between 38% and 39%. As Apple's profitable services business grew, its gross margin expanded, hitting 40% in 2021 and growing from there.

Analysts see a potential silver lining for Apple in the current market turmoil. Because Apple is more capable of eating a portion of the increased costs, some experts predict the company will take market share from makers of Windows PC and Android phones that don't have such a cushion.

Citing data from a third-party researcher, Ternus said gains are already happening.

"According to IDC, we gained share globally for the quarter and for the full fiscal year," Ternus said. "And we have never sold more Macs in a fiscal year than we did this time, which is a truly remarkable testament to these incredible products."

#2868. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2866. Fantasy football Start ‘Em, Sit ‘Em: 16 players to start or sit in Week 9

After several four-team byes, NFL fans will be treated to a simple two-team bye in Week 9.

The Pittsburgh Steelers and Tennessee Titans will be the two teams that get the week off. That will leave fantasy managers searching for replacements for the likes of Jaylen Warren, Tony Pollard and DK Metcalf, among others, but there should be extra streaming depth with 30 of the league's teams in action.

Of course, the bigger issue for many fantasy football managers will be finding a replacement for Lamar Jackson. The star quarterback lasted just one snap in his return from an ankle injury before suffering a thumb injury that could be season-ending.

Luckily, a couple of great-looking streaming options are available this week for fantasy managers to target on the waiver wire.

Who can you start and sit in fantasy football for Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season? USA TODAY Sports outlines eight players to start and eight to sit:

Malik Willis, Miami Dolphins (vs. Detroit Lions)

Willis is starting to round into form for the Dolphins and has scored multiple touchdowns in three consecutive games. During that span, he has averaged 37.7 rushing yards per game, which will raise his floor in most matchups. The Lions have allowed the most fantasy points per game (FPPG) to quarterbacks this season, so Willis should be positioned to have a strong showing in what figures to be a favorable, high-scoring matchup.

Sam Darnold, Seattle Seahawks (vs. Arizona Cardinals)

The Cardinals are one of just three NFL teams to allow 18 passing touchdowns to quarterbacks this season, along with the Lions and the Las Vegas Raiders. Unsurprisingly, Arizona has allowed a league-high 29.8 because of that, so Darnold should be positioned for a nice outing in which he could toss multiple touchdowns.

Quinshon Judkins, Cleveland Browns (at New Orleans Saints)

Judkins has been boom-or-bust as a touchdown-dependent fantasy option, but he should be poised for a good outing against the Saints. New Orleans has allowed the most FPPG to running backs this season, including a league-high 11 rushing touchdowns to the position. With that in mind, Judkins should be able to find his way into the end zone.

Jonah Coleman, Denver Broncos (at Carolina Panthers)

Coleman took on a lion's share of Denver's backfield touches in Week 9, posting 104 total yards and a touchdown on 15 touches (11 carries, four receptions). The rookie should continue in that role and has a great matchup against a Panthers defense that has allowed the second-most FPPG to running backs this season.

Caleb Douglas, Miami Dolphins (vs. Detroit Lions)

As mentioned, the Lions have been weak against the pass this year. If we're recommending to start Willis, then Douglas should get a nod too, as the rookie has notched six receptions for 113 yards and two touchdowns in two games since returning from an early-season ankle injury.

Ladd McConkey, Los Angeles Chargers (vs. Houston Texans)

Trusting McConkey may seem risky, given that Justin Herbert has struggled under pressure this season. That said, the Texans are allowing the second-most FPPG to receivers this season and McConkey is fresh off a four-catch, 86-yard outing that was one of his best of the season. He should be in line for a solid showing against the Texans.

Mike Gesicki, Cincinnati Bengals (at Atlanta Falcons)

Gesicki has scored in five consecutive games and six of the seven he has played this season. Joe Burrow has thrown 13 touchdown passes over his last three games, so Gesicki should continue to have high touchdown upside regardless of his opponent.

Jacksonville Jaguars (at Baltimore Ravens)

The Jaguars are playing the Ravens on a short week. Baltimore is expected to start third-string quarterback Joe Fagnano, an undrafted rookie, after injuries to Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley. Fagnano completed 10 of 21 passes for 91 yards and an interception while being sacked three times against the Bills. The Jaguars should pressure him into numerous mistakes while their top-ranked run defense should be able to bottle up Derrick Henry.

Bryce Young, Carolina Panthers (vs. Denver Broncos)

Young has regressed over the last two weeks after a torrid start to the season, completing just 54.4% of his passes for 372 yards, two touchdowns and three interceptions during Carolina's two-game losing skid. The main issue? Young has been pressured a lot in those contests, and he has just a 64 passer rating when pressured compared to 106.3 when kept clean, per Pro Football Focus. The Broncos rank fifth in pressure rate league-wide, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats, so they should be able to limit Young in this favorable spot.

Bo Nix, Denver Broncos (at Carolina Panthers)

The Panthers have a banged-up secondary, but it still won't be easy to justify starting Nix. The Broncos quarterback has looked better recently, throwing for exactly 222 yards in back-to-back games and scoring twice in each. Still, the Panthers are typically more susceptible against the run, so Nix has a lower ceiling than most streaming options this week.

Ashton Jeanty, Las Vegas Raiders (at San Francisco 49ers)

Can you really trust Jeanty after the Raiders gave him just two carries against the Jets while giving Mike Washington Jr. a whopping 25? Jeanty will remain productive as a receiver, but his uncertain between-the-tackles workload makes it hard to trust the former first-round pick, especially against a 49ers defense allowing just 3.6 yards per carry to running backs this season. It's better to wait and see if Jeanty's decrease in carries was a punishment for trash-talking the Jets before Week 8 or a sign of things to come in Klint Kubiak's running back rotation.

Breece Hall, New York Jets (at Kansas City Chiefs)

Hall seems likely to return from a nagging quad injury this week, but he gets a tough matchup against a Chiefs defense that has largely bottled up opposing running backs this season. Hall may also be splitting carries with Braelon Allen, who has thrived in his stead, so this doesn't seem like an ideal spot to trust him.

Michael Wilson, Arizona Cardinals (at Seattle Seahawks)

Wilson finally had a breakout game for the first time since Marvin Harrison Jr. was traded, generating eight catches for 111 yards and a touchdown against the Cowboys. He'll have a much tougher matchup against a Seahawks team that has been excellent against receivers this year, especially after Wilson suffered a couple of broken ribs on his touchdown catch.

Adonai Mitchell, New York Jets (at Kansas City Chiefs)

Mitchell just logged seven catches for 142 yards a touchdown against the Raiders in Week 8. He gets a much tougher matchup in Week 9 against a Chiefs defense that has allowed the second-fewest FPPG to receivers this season. Add in that Omar Cooper Jr. could also siphon targets from him and Mitchell may be a hit-or-miss play each week.

Dalton Kincaid, Buffalo Bills (at Minnesota Vikings)

Kincaid scored a touchdown last week for the first time since Week 2, but he will be hard-pressed to repeat that feat against the Vikings. Minnesota has yet to allow a receiving touchdown to tight ends this season, so Kincaid figures to be limited.

No NFL team has allowed fewer points per game than the Vikings (13.3). No NFL team has scored more points per game than the Bills (33.3). Something will give in this matchup, but if there's one quarterback that's built to beat Brian Flores' pressure-happy scheme, it's Josh Allen.

#2865. U.S. crude oil falls below $95 per barrel as market hopes for diplomatic resolution to Iran war

Crude oil prices fell Tuesday for a second consecutive session, as the market hopes the U.S. and Iran will reach a diplomatic settlement to the eight-month-long conflict that has disrupted global energy supplies.

Brent crude was down 3% at $101.54 per barrel, while West Texas Intermediate was 2.3% lower to $94.23. Oil prices sold off sharply Monday after the U.S. and Iran signaled they are open to diplomacy.

But the risk of escalation persists. Washington attacked Iran's Qeshm Island on Monday, U.S. officials told The Wall Street Journal, adding that the U.S. has been carrying out "limited strikes" against the country in recent weeks.

The military campaign against Iran has been kept deliberately low-profile to avoid signaling plans for a major escalation in fighting, the report said, quoting an unnamed official.

But despite these strikes, the Trump administration has also been touting a possible diplomatic solution to the conflict, with Vice President JD Vance telling CNN, "The president and I both feel really good about the prospect of potentially coming to a negotiated settlement."

On Sunday, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said President Donald Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

High oil prices have contributed substantially to inflation and high gasoline prices in the U.S., which have become a cause of concern for Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

Last week, two Federal Reserve presidents said interest rates will need to move higher to rein in inflation. Higher rates generally crimp growth and raise costs for consumers, but help to bring down inflation.
